“的确，我担保您这回是全然错了，”温特沃斯先生慌忙说道，脸上蓦地涨起红来，也说不清是恼怒还是心虚。副牧师（curate）朝露西·伍德豪斯（Lucy Wodehouse）瞟了一眼；露西正端端正正地走在他身旁，可是这桩小小的新闻，她分明是竖起了耳朵的。她看得清清楚楚，他望了她一眼，却偏要摆出不理会这一瞟的样子。然而露西的好奇心，毕竟显然叫这个消息给撩动起来了；圣罗克小屋（St. Roque's Cottage）离着近便得很——万一这座近郊漂亮教堂的常驻副牧师（perpetual curate）看出那儿有什么值得走访的去处呢。露西裹着她那件灰色的仁爱姊妹式斗篷，把好看的肩头微微一端，一双蓝眼睛也睁得略大了些。倘若他那双眼睛当真从她身上滑落，去垂青什么比她低一等的妙处，她仍有的是法子叫她那位可敬的情人不敢放肆。她目不斜视，带着一种毫不容情的精确，直直望着大路前方——圣罗克教堂（St. Roque's Church）和圣罗克小屋，已经遥遥在望了。这一行人全都踏着冻硬的小径，脚底下咚咚作响，脚下生风地走着。空气里带着一点儿冬意，静悄悄的，寒气把万物冻得发木；然而每一种声响，都从那澄澈无云的大气里铮然回荡；西沉的太阳把天光染得泛红，却透不出半点暖意。圣诞节又到了，他们正一路往圣罗克去，帮着张罗节日的装点——成车成车的月桂和冬青，早已卸进教堂里头了。这几桩要紧的大事，露西·伍德豪斯是总指挥。她的姐姐一路作伴同来，一半是为了赞赏露西的手艺，一半是想顺路到小屋里去，看看妮蒂（Nettie）近来的光景。伍德豪斯先生自己跑这一趟，单单是为着那份得意和欢喜——要亲眼看看人家多么亏欠他这位小姑娘的情分；至于副牧师的大驾光临，那更是顶好解释不过的了。说来实在叫人惊异，竟有那么桩桩件件万分必要、万分自然的“职分所系”，把温特沃斯先生的路，引得同伍德豪斯家的人们平行并进。这就是为什么，圣诞前那个星期里的这个特定下午，他们全都一同步行，往圣罗克去了。

===Original===

In the church, when the party arrived, a little group of workers were busy. The chancel arch was already bristling with glossy holly-leaves. At a little distance from the active group occupied with this pleasant work, and full of chatter and consultation, as was natural, stood one little figure pointing out to two children the wonders of that decorative art. Every one of the newcomers, except Mr. Wodehouse, recognised Nettie before she was aware of their presence. She stood with her bonnet fallen a little back, as it generally was, either by encounter of the wind, or by the quantity and luxuriance of her beautiful hair, looking upwards to the point where she had directed the children’s eyes. She looked a little forlorn and solitary, as was natural, all by herself, so near that group of busy girls in the chancel﻿—so little separated from them by age, so entirely divided by circumstances. If a certain softening of half-tender pity shone in the curate’s eye, could Lucy Wodehouse blame him? But the fact was, Lucy swept past the little Australian with a very brief salutation, and burst into sudden criticism of the work that had been done in her absence, which startled her collaborateurs, while Mr. Wentworth followed her into the chancel with a meekness quite unusual to that young priest. Nettie noted both circumstances with a little surprise; but, not connecting them in the most distant degree with herself, turned round with a little twitch of Freddy’s arm to go away, and in doing so almost walked into the arms of her older and more faithful friend. Miss Wodehouse kissed her quite suddenly, touching with her soft old cheek that rounder, fairer, youthful face, which turned, half wondering, half pleased, with the look of a child, to receive her caress. Nettie was as unconscious that Miss Wodehouse’s unusual warmth was meant to make up for Lucy’s careless greeting, as that Lucy had passed her with a positive flutter of resentment and indignation, and that she had been the subject of the conversation and thoughts of all the party. Miss Wodehouse turned with her, taking Freddy’s other hand﻿—a proceeding to which that hero rather demurred. They went out together to the frosty road, where the bare willow-branches rustled between the church and the cottage. When they reached the porch of St. Roque’s, Nettie instinctively held her breath, and stood still for a moment. Along the footpath in front of them a big figure was passing, and beyond that bearded shadow the doctor’s drag flew past with all the separate tones of the horse’s feet, the wheels, the jingle of the harness, ringing clear through the sharp unsoftened medium of that frosty atmosphere. The doctor himself had all his attention concentrated upon the windows of the cottage, in which the sun was blazing red. He did not see Nettie in the church porch. He was looking for her too intently in the crimsoned windows, to which he turned his head back as he dashed on. Unawares Nettie clasped the fingers of her little companion tighter in her hand as she watched that unexpected homage. The drag was out of sight in another moment; and in a few seconds more the bell of the cottage pealed audibly, and the door was heard to open, admitting the Bushman, who had come upon one of his frequent visits. That last sound disturbed Nettie’s composure, and at the same time brought her back to herself.

===Chinese===

这一行人走进教堂的时候，里头已有一小簇干活的人忙开了。圣坛的拱门上，已经竖满了油亮光润的冬青叶子。离着那群欢欢喜喜做这桩美差、自然也叽叽喳喳商量个不停的热闹人儿不远处，立着一个小小的身影，正指给两个孩子看这门装饰艺术里的种种奇观。新来的人里，除了伍德豪斯先生，个个都在妮蒂察觉他们到来之前，先认出了她。她立在那里，帽子照例往后褪了些——不是迎着风给掀的，便是叫她那头又浓又密的丰茂美发给蹭的——仰着脸，望着她方才引孩子们眼睛去看的那个地方。她看上去有几分凄惶孤单，这原也自然：孤零零一个人，同圣坛里那群忙忙碌碌的姑娘离得那么近——论年岁同她们相去无几，论境遇却分隔得干干净净。倘若副牧师的眼里闪过一丝半温半柔的怜惜的软化，露西·伍德豪斯又岂能怪他？然而事实是，露西打那个小小的澳洲来客身旁一掠而过，只匆匆招呼了一声，便劈头盖脸，突然对她不在的时候干出来的活计发起一连串批评，把她的合作者们吓了一跳；温特沃斯先生呢，则带着一种对那位年轻牧师来说实在罕见的温顺，跟在她身后进了圣坛。这两桩情形，妮蒂都略略有点诧异地看在眼里；可是她做梦也没把它们同自己牵连到一处，只回过身，轻轻一拽弗雷迪（Freddy）的胳膊要走，就在这转身之间，险些一头撞进她那位年纪更长、也更为忠心的朋友的怀里。伍德豪斯小姐冷不防吻了她，拿自己温软的苍老面颊，贴了贴那张更圆润、更白皙的青春脸庞；那张脸半是诧异半是欢喜，带着孩子的神情，转过来承受她的爱抚。伍德豪斯小姐这一反常的热乎劲儿，本意是要补一补露西那声草草的招呼；露西打她身边过去时，分明还带着一股实实在在的愠怒不平的颤动；而她自己，竟是这一行人谈话和心思的总题目——对这一切，妮蒂全然无所知觉。伍德豪斯小姐转身同她一道走，牵起弗雷迪的另一只手——对这番举措，那位小英雄颇不以为然。两人一同走出去，到了结霜的大路上；教堂和小屋之间，光秃秃的柳条飒飒作响。走到圣罗克教堂的门廊跟前，妮蒂不自觉地屏住呼吸，站住不动了。他们前面的小径上，一个大个子的身影正走过去；隔着那道络腮胡子的影子，医生的马车飞驰而过——马蹄声、车轮声、马具上铃铛的叮当声，声声分明，透过那冻得又尖利又生硬、毫不柔和的空气，清清楚楚地传了过来。医生自己呢，全副心神都凝在小屋的窗户上，窗玻璃里正烧着通红的落日。他没有看见教堂门廊里的妮蒂。他在那一扇扇染红的窗户里找她找得太专注了，马车疾驰过去，他还回过头去张望。妮蒂望着这一番料想不到的致敬，不知不觉把身边小伙伴的手指攥得更紧了。转眼间马车就没影了；再过几秒钟，小屋的门铃叮叮当当地响起来，听得真真的，跟着听见门开了，把那位丛林客（Bushman）让了进去——他是来登门造访的，这样的造访他常来常往。最后这声响，搅乱了妮蒂的镇定，同时又把她拉回到她自己身上来。

===Original===

“I cannot ask you to go in, for Mr. Chatham is there, and Susan of course talking to him,” said Nettie, with a quiet breath of restrained impatience, “but I should like to talk to you, please. Let me take the children home, and then I will walk up with you. Mrs. Smith is very kind; she will take off their things for them: they behave better now, when I am out for a few minutes﻿—though, to be sure, I never am out much to try them. Come, children; be good, and do not make a great noise till I come back.”

“What do you want to talk to *her* for?” asked the little girl, gazing coldly in Miss Wodehouse’s face.

“When Nettie went out to tea, we made as much noise as we liked,” said Freddy, “but there was papa there. Now there’s only mamma, and she’s so cross. I hate Chatham﻿—mamma is always crossest when Chatham’s there. What do you want to talk to people for, Nettie? Come in, and say there’s to be toast, and let us have tea.”

“We never have any tea till Nettie comes back,” added his sister, looking full once more into Miss Wodehouse’s face. The calm childish impertinence disconcerted that gentle woman. She gazed at the wonderful creatures with dumb amazement. Her eyes fell before their steady stare. “I should be sorry to bring you out again, dear, if it’s a trouble,” began Miss Wodehouse, turning her face with a sense of relief from the hard inspection of the children to their little guardian.

Nettie made no reply, but carried off her children to the cottage door, turned them peremptorily in, and issued her last orders. “If you make a noise, you shall not go,” said Nettie; and then came back alert, with her rapid fairy steps, to Miss Wodehouse’s side.

===Chinese===

“我不能请您进去，因为查塔姆先生（Mr. Chatham）在屋里，苏珊（Susan）自然得陪他说话，”妮蒂说，轻轻透出一口按捺住的不耐烦的气，“可是我很想同您说说话，劳驾您等一等。让我先把孩子们送回家，然后我再陪您一路走上去。史密斯太太（Mrs. Smith）心肠很好，会替他们把外衣脱下来安置好：如今我出门那几分钟里，他们规矩些了——不过说实在的，我也不大出门，没怎么试过他们。来，孩子们；乖一点，我回来之前不许大声吵嚷。”

“你要同*她*说话做什么？”小姑娘冷冷地盯着伍德豪斯小姐的脸，问道。

“当初妮蒂出门喝茶去的时候，我们爱怎么闹就怎么闹，”弗雷迪说，“不过那时候有爸爸在。如今就剩妈妈一个人，她又凶得很。我讨厌查塔姆——查塔姆一来，妈妈就凶得顶了。妮蒂，你同人家说话，有什么可说的？进来吧，就说有烤面包片吃，咱们好喝茶啦。”

“妮蒂不回来，我们从来喝不上茶，”他姐姐又补了一句，再一次直直地盯住伍德豪斯小姐的脸。这份孩子家不动声色的无礼，把那位温柔的太太弄得手足无措了。她张口结舌，又惊又愕地望着这两个奇怪的小东西，眼光在那两道目不转睛的逼视下垂了下去。“要是这叫你为难，亲爱的，我真不愿意再把你带出门来了，”伍德豪斯小姐开口说道，带着如释重负的心情，把脸从孩子们那种硬邦邦的打量底下转开，朝他们的小监护人那边去。

妮蒂没有答话，只把她的孩子们一把带走，径直送到小屋门口，不由分说地把他们打发进去，下了一道最后的命令。“你们要是吵闹，就不许去了，”妮蒂说；随即脚步轻捷，迈着她那种急急的小仙子步子，麻利地回到伍德豪斯小姐身边。

===Original===

“Does not their mother take any charge of them?” faltered the gentle inquisitor. “I never can understand you young people, Nettie. Things were different in my days. Do you think it’s quite the best thing to do other people’s duties for them, dear? and now I’m so sorry﻿—oh, so sorry﻿—to hear what you are going to do now.”

“Susan is delicate,” said Nettie. “She never had any health to speak of﻿—I mean, she always got better, you know, but never had any pleasure in it. There must be a great deal in that,” continued Nettie, reflectively; “it never comes into my head to think whether I am ill or well; but poor Susan has always had to be thinking of it. Yes, I shall have to take them away,” she added again, after a pause. “I am sorry, very sorry too, Miss Wodehouse. I did not think at one time that I had the heart to do it. But on the whole, you know, it seems so much better for them. Susan will be stronger out there, and I have not money enough to give the children a very good education. They will just have to push their way like the others; and in the colony, you know, things are so different. I have no doubt in my own mind now that it will be best for them all.”

“But, Nettie, Nettie, what of yourself? will it be best for you?” cried Miss Wodehouse, looking earnestly in her face.

“What is best for them will be best for me,” said Nettie, with a little impatient movement of her head. She said so with unfaltering spirit and promptitude. She had come to be impatient of the dreary maze in which she was involved. “If one must break one’s heart, it is best to do it at once and have done with it,” said Nettie, under her breath.

===Chinese===

“他们的母亲就一点也不管他们吗？”那位温柔的盘问人嗫嚅着说。“你们这些年轻人，我实在弄不懂，妮蒂。我那个年月，事情可不是这样。你当替旁人把本分包办下来，就是顶好的做法吗，亲爱的？如今听说你眼下要做的事，我心里难过极了——哦，难过极了。”

“苏珊身子单薄，”妮蒂说。“她从来谈不上有什么健康可言——我是说，她一回回总能见好，你知道，可是见好了也从来没有快活过。这里头必定大有讲究，”妮蒂沉吟着接下去说；“我脑子里从来不去想自己是有病还是没病；可怜的苏珊却一向不得不老想着这件事。是啊，我总得把他们带走，”隔了一会儿，她又说道。“我也难过，非常难过，伍德豪斯小姐。从前有一阵子，我还当自己没有这份狠心来做这件事呢。可是总的说来，你知道，这对他们要好多啦。苏珊到了那边，身子会结实些；我又没有足够的钱，供孩子们受多好的教育。他们到头来也只好同旁人一样自己闯出路来；而在殖民地上，你知道，情形大不相同。如今我自己心里已经不怀疑了：这样对他们大家都是最好的。”

“可是，妮蒂，妮蒂，你自己呢？这对你也是最好的吗？”伍德豪斯小姐嚷道，恳切地端详着她的脸。

“对他们最好的，对我也就是最好的，”妮蒂说，头微微一动，带着几分不耐。她说这话的时候，意气坚定，毫不迟疑。对于缠住她的那座凄凉的迷阵，她已渐渐耐不住了。“人若横竖要心碎，顶好是立刻碎个痛快，从此拉倒，”妮蒂低声说。

===Original===

“What was that you said about your heart?” said Miss Wodehouse. “Ah, my dear, that is what I wanted to speak of. You are going to be married, Nettie, and I wanted to suggest to you, if you won’t be angry. Don’t you think you could make some arrangement about your sister and her family, dear?﻿—not to say a word against the Australian gentleman, Nettie, whom, of course, I don’t know. A man may be the best of husbands, and yet not be able to put up with a whole family. I have no doubt the children are very nice clever children, but their manner is odd, you know, for such young creatures. You have been sacrificing yourself for them all this time; but remember what I say﻿—if you want to live happily, my dear, you’ll have to sacrifice them to your husband. I could not be content without saying as much to you, Nettie. I never was half the good in this world that you are, but I am nearly twice as old﻿—and one does pick up some little hints on the way. That is what you must do, Nettie. Make some arrangement, dear. If he has promised to take them out with you, that is all right enough; but when you come to settle down in your new home, make some arrangement dear.”

===Chinese===

“你方才说你那颗心，说了句什么来着？”伍德豪斯小姐说。“啊，亲爱的，我正要同你讲的就是这个。你要结婚了，妮蒂，我想给你提个醒儿，只要你不恼。你替你姐姐和她一家人作个安排出来，你不觉得才是好办法吗，亲爱的？——这倒不是说那位澳大利亚来的先生半个字的坏话，妮蒂，他那个人，我自然不认识。一个人尽可以做天下顶好的丈夫，却未必受得了一家子人。孩子们自然都是很好很聪明的孩子，这我毫不怀疑；可是你知道，这么小小的年纪，他们的做派总有点古怪。你为他们牺牲自己，牺牲了这么久；可是记住我的话——你若想过得快活，亲爱的，你就得把他们牺牲给你的丈夫。不把这话同你说，妮蒂，我心里过不去。我在这个世上行的善，从来不及你的一半，可是年纪差不多长你一倍——人一路走来，总拾得着些小见识。你就该这么办，妮蒂。作个安排，亲爱的。他要是应许过连孩子们一道带出去，那自然再好没有；可是等你到了新家里安顿下来，就得作个安排，亲爱的。”

===Original===

When Miss Wodehouse arrived breathless at the conclusion of a speech so unusually long for her, she met Nettie’s eyes flashing upon her with the utmost surprise and curiosity. “I shall never marry anybody,” said Nettie. “What do you mean?”

“Don’t say anything so foolish,” said Miss Wodehouse, a little nettled. “Do you suppose I don’t know and see *that* Mr. Chatham coming and going? How often has he been seen since the first time, Nettie? and do you suppose it’s all been benevolence? My dear, I know better.”

Nettie looked up with a startled glance. She did not blush, nor betray any pleasant consciousness. She cast one dismayed look back towards the cottage, and another at Miss Wodehouse. “Can *that* be why he comes?” said Nettie, with quiet horror. “Indeed, I never thought of it before﻿—but all the same, I shall never marry anybody. Do you imagine,” cried the brilliant creature, flashing round upon poor Miss Wodehouse, so as to dazzle and confuse that gentlewoman, “that a man has only to intend such a thing and it’s all settled? I think differently. Twenty thousand Chathams would not move me. I shall never marry anybody, if I live to be as old as﻿—as you, or Methuselah, or anybody. It is not my lot. I shall take the children out to Australia, and do the best I can for them. Three children want a great deal of looking after﻿—and after a while in Carlingford, you will all forget that there ever was such a creature as Nettie. No, I am not crying. I never cry. I should scorn to cry about it. It is simply *my business*. That is what it is. One is sorry, of course, and now and then it feels hard, and all that. But what did one come into the world for, I should like to know? Does anybody suppose it was just to be comfortable, and have one’s own way? I have had my own way a great deal﻿—more than most people. If I get crossed in some things, I have to bear it. That is all I am going to say. I have got other things to do, Miss Wodehouse. I shall never marry anybody all my life.”

===Chinese===

伍德豪斯小姐说完这篇于她实在破例的长篇大论，喘着气到了头，迎面撞上的却是妮蒂闪射过来的眼睛，那里面有说不尽的惊讶和好奇。“我谁也不嫁，”妮蒂说。“您这话是什么意思？”

“别说这样的话，太傻了，”伍德豪斯小姐说，微微有些着恼。“你当我不认得、看不出*那位*查塔姆先生进进出出吗？打头一回之后，人家瞧见他来过多少回啦，妮蒂？你当那全是发善心吗？亲爱的，我可是知道得多着呢。”

妮蒂猛地抬起头来，眼里一惊。她没有红脸，也没有流露出半点得意的羞喜。她惶惑地朝小屋的方向回望了一眼，又望了望伍德豪斯小姐。“难道*那就是*他来的缘故吗？”妮蒂说，静静的声气里含着惊骇。“说真的，我从前想也没想到过——不过尽管这样，我谁也不嫁。您当，”这个光彩四射的小家伙嚷道，霍地转过身来对着可怜的伍德豪斯小姐，直晃得那位温文的老小姐眼花缭乱、心慌意乱，“您当一个男人只要起了这么个念头，事情就十拿九稳了吗？我的想法可不是这样。两万个查塔姆也打动不了我。我就是活到像您这样的岁数，或者活到玛土撒拉（Methuselah），或者随便谁的岁数，也决计谁也不嫁。这不是我的命。我要带孩子们上澳大利亚去，为他们竭尽我的所能。三个孩子，要操的心可多着呢——再过些时候，在卡林福德（Carlingford），你们人人都会忘掉，世上曾经有过妮蒂这么一个活人。没有，我没有哭。我从来不哭。为这个掉眼泪，我不屑。这不过是*我的本分*。就是这么一回事。人自然有难过的时候，偶尔也觉得难，诸如此类。可是我想请问，一个人到这世上来，究竟是为了什么来的？难道有谁当真以为，来这一遭就只是为着舒舒服服、遂自己的心意？我遂心如意的时候可多着呢——比多数人都多。有些事情上头受了挫，我就得受着。我要说的话，统共就这些。我还有别的事要办呢，伍德豪斯小姐。我这辈子，谁也不嫁。”

===Original===

“My dear, if you are thrown upon this Mr. Chatham for society all the time of the voyage, and have nobody else to talk to﻿—” said the prudent interlocutor.

“Then we’ll go in another ship,” cried Nettie, promptly; “that is easily managed. I know what it is, a long voyage with three children﻿—they fall up the cabin-stairs, and they fall down the forecastle; and they give you twenty frights in a day that they will drop overboard. One does not have much leisure for anything﻿—not even for thinking, which is a comfort sometimes,” added Nettie, confidentially, to herself.

“It depends upon what you think of, whether thinking is a comfort or not,” said good Miss Wodehouse. “When I think of you young people, and all the perplexities you get into! There is Lucy now, vexed with Mr. Wentworth about something﻿—oh, nothing worth mentioning; and there was poor Dr. Rider! How he did look behind him, to be sure, as he went past St. Roque’s! I daresay it was you he was looking for, Nettie. I wish you and he could have fancied each other, and come to some arrangement about poor Mr. Fred’s family﻿—to give them so much to live on, or something. I assure you, when I begin to think over such things, and how perverse both people and circumstances are, thinking is very little comfort to me.”

===Chinese===

“可是亲爱的，要是整整一路航程，你都只得靠这位查塔姆先生作伴说话，再没有旁人——”这位审慎的对话者说。

“那我们就搭另一条船走，”妮蒂应声答道，“这还不容易办。什么样的滋味我知道——带着三个孩子过漫漫的长海路，他们顺着舱梯一路摔上去，又朝水手舱里滚下去；一天教你受二十场虚惊，生怕他们翻过船舷掉进海里去。人没有多少闲工夫顾别的——连想心思的工夫都没有，这有时候倒也算一种安慰，”妮蒂又添了这么一句，悄悄的，像是知心话只说给她自己听。

“想心思算不算安慰，得看你想的是什么，”善良的伍德豪斯小姐说。“我一想到你们这些年轻人，想到你们搅进去的这许多为难事！就说露西吧，这会儿正为一点事同温特沃斯先生怄着气——哦，不值一提的小事；还有可怜的莱德医生（Dr. Rider）呢！他从圣罗克跟前过去的时候，那个回头张望的劲儿，可不是玩的！我敢说，他找的就是你，妮蒂。但愿你们两个能彼此看得对眼，替可怜的弗雷德先生（Mr. Fred）一家人立个章程——给他们一笔数目做生活费，或者想个旁的法子。老实对你说，我一想到这类事情，一想到人和境遇都这样别扭作对，想心思于我可就没多少安慰了。”

===Original===

Miss Wodehouse drew a long sigh, and was by no means disinclined to cry over her little companion. Though she was the taller of the two, she leant upon Nettie’s firm little fairy arm as they went up the quiet road. Already the rapid winter twilight had fallen, and before them, in the distance, glimmered the lights of Carlingford﻿—foremost among which shone conspicuous the large placid white lamp (for professional reds and blues were beneath his dignity) which mounted guard at Dr. Marjoribanks’s garden gate. Those lights, beginning to shine through the evening darkness, gave a wonderful look of home to the place. Instinctively there occurred to Nettie’s mind a vision of how it would be on the sea, with a wide dark ocean heaving around the solitary speck on its breast. It did not matter! If a silent sob arose in her heart, it found no utterance. Might not Edward Rider have made that suggestion which had occurred only to Miss Wodehouse? Why did it never come into his head that Susan and her family might have a provision supplied for them, which would relieve Nettie? He had not thought of it, that was all. Instead of that, he had accepted the impossibility. Nettie’s heart had grown impatient in the maze of might-be’s. She turned her back upon the lights, and clasped Miss Wodehouse’s hand, and said good night hastily. She went on by herself very rapidly along the hard gleaming road. She did not pay any attention to her friend’s protestation that she too was coming back again to St. Roque’s to join Lucy﻿—on the contrary, Nettie peremptorily left Miss Wodehouse, shaking hands with her in so resolute a manner that her gentle adviser felt somehow a kind of necessity upon her to pursue her way home; and, only when Nettie was nearly out of sight, turned again with hesitation to retrace her steps towards St. Roque’s. Nettie, meanwhile, went on at a pace which Miss Wodehouse could not possibly have kept up with, clasping her tiny hands together with a swell of scorn and disdain unusual to it in her heart. Yes! Why did not Edward Rider propose the “arrangement” which appeared feasible enough to Miss Wodehouse? Supposing even Nettie had refused to consent to it, as she might very probably have done with indignation﻿—still, why did it not occur to Dr. Edward? She asked herself the question with a heat and passion which she found it difficult to account for. She half despised her lover, as woman will, for obeying her﻿—almost scorned him, as woman will, for the mere constancy which took no violent measures, but only suffered and accepted the inevitable. To submit to what cannot be helped is a woman’s part. Nettie, hastening along that familiar path, blazed into a sudden burst of rage against Edward because he submitted. What he could do else she was as ignorant of as any unreasonable creature could be. But that mattered little. With indignation she saw herself standing on the verge of that domestic precipice, and the doctor looking on, seeing her glide out of his reach, yet putting forth no violent sudden hand to detain her. All the impatience of her fiery nature boiled in her veins as she hasted to the cottage, where Susan was discussing their journey with her Australian visitor. No remnant of pathos or love-sickening remained about Nettie, as she flashed in upon them in all her old haste and self-reliance﻿—resolute to precipitate the catastrophe which nobody took any measures to prevent.

===Chinese===

伍德豪斯小姐长长地叹了一口气，对着她这位小朋友，原也未必不愿意哭上一场。两人里数她个子高，可走上那条静悄悄的大路时，她却倚着妮蒂那条结实的小仙子胳膊。冬天来去匆匆的暮色已经落下来了；前面远处，卡林福德的灯火闪烁着——其中最惹眼的，是那盏守在马杰里班克斯医生（Dr. Marjoribanks）园门口的安详的大白灯（职业医生的那点红红蓝蓝，原不配他的身份）。这些灯火开始从入夜的黑暗里透出光来，给这地方平添了一种说不出的家的意味。妮蒂心里不自觉地浮起一幅幻景：在大海上将会是什么光景——一片广阔黑暗的大洋起伏翻涌，环抱着浮在它胸前那个孤零零的小点。那也不要紧！她心里纵使悄悄地哽咽了一声，也没有宣泄出来。爱德华·莱德（Edward Rider）怎么就不能提出那个主意来呢——那主意，也只有伍德豪斯小姐一个人想到了。他怎么就从来不曾想到，苏珊和她一家人未尝不可以有人给备下一份供给，好叫妮蒂解脱出来？他没有想过，如此而已。他反倒把那桩不可能的事认了下来。妮蒂的心，在这座由“或许”结成的迷阵里，已经熬得不耐烦了。她掉转身，背对灯火，攥住伍德豪斯小姐的手，匆匆道了晚安。她独自一个，顺着那条冻得发亮的大路飞快地走去。她的朋友再三声明自己原是要回圣罗克去同露西会合的，她根本不理会——恰恰相反，妮蒂不由分说地撇下了伍德豪斯小姐，同她握手握得那样斩钉截铁，倒叫她那位温厚的参谋自己觉得，仿佛有一股不得不然的力压在身上，催着她一路回家去；直到妮蒂几乎看不见了，才又迟疑着转过身，重新朝圣罗克那边踱回去。这当儿，妮蒂一路走下去，那步子是伍德豪斯小姐说什么也追不上的；她两只小手紧紧攥在一处，心里涌起一股于她颇为少见的轻蔑与鄙夷。可不是！爱德华·莱德怎么就不提那个在伍德豪斯小姐看来分明行得通的“安排”呢？就算妮蒂多半会愤然不肯答应——那也大有可能——可是，爱德华医生（Dr. Edward）怎么就想不起这一层呢？她问着自己这个问题，心头一股热辣辣的火气，连她自己都觉得难以解释。她一半瞧不起她的情人——女人家总是这样——嫌他一味依顺她；几乎还鄙薄他——女人家也总是这样——就为他那份安分的坚贞：不使任何激烈手段，只是一味隐忍，把无可如何的事承受下来。屈从于无可如何的事，原是女人的本分。妮蒂沿着那条走熟了的小路急急地赶，却因为他这样屈从，蓦地朝爱德华发起一阵无名怒火。除了屈从他还能做什么，她同一切不通情理的生灵一样，一无所知。可这无关紧要。她愤愤地看着自己立在那道家事悬崖的边上，医生在一旁望着，眼睁睁瞧她滑出他力所能及的地方，却始终不伸出一只断然的手来把她留住。她急急往小屋赶去——屋里苏珊正同她那位澳大利亚来的客人商量着他们一家的行期——她那烈性子里全部的不耐烦，在血脉里滚滚地沸着。她带着往日那股急脾气和全凭自己的气概，一闪身闯到他们跟前时，身上已经一点缠绵悱恻的余痕都没有了——她一心要催快那场大祸，而谁也不曾采取任何手段去拦阻它。

### XV / 十五

===Original===
It was not long before the doctor was made aware of the ghost in his troubled path. Nobody in Carlingford could meet the big Bushman in those streets, which always looked too narrow for him, without a certain curiosity about that salvage man. Dr. Rider had observed him with jealous interest on his very first appearance, but had hitherto connected no idea but that of a return to Australia, which he felt sure Nettie would never consent to, with the big stranger. With such a thought he had seen him making his way towards the cottage that very evening when he himself turned back, as long as those crimsoned windows were visible, to look for Nettie, who did not show herself. The doctor was bound to see a distant patient, miles on the other side of Carlingford. As he dashed along over the echoing road he had time to imagine to himself how Nettie might at that very moment be badgered and persecuted; and when he had seen his patient and done his duty, and with the lamps lighted in the drag, and the frosty wind blowing keen on his face, and the lights of Carlingford cheering him on in the distance, was once more returning, an impatience, somewhat akin to Nettie’s, suddenly came upon the doctor. Akin, yet different; for in his case it was an impulse of sensation, an inspiration of the exhilarating speed and energy of motion with which he flew through the bracing air, master of himself, his horse, and the long sweep of solitary road before him. Again it occurred to Dr. Rider to dash forward to St. Roque’s and carry off Nettie, oppose it who would. The idea pleased him as he swept along in the darkness, its very impossibility making the vision sweeter. To carry her off at a stroke, in glorious defiance of circumstances, and win happiness and love, whatever might ensue. In the flush of the moment the doctor suddenly asked himself whether this, after all, were not the wisest course? whether, whatever might come of it, happiness was not worth the encounter of the dark array of troubles behind? and whether to precipitate everything by a sudden conclusion might not be the best way of solving all the intricacies of the matter? He was still in this mood when he arrived at his own house, where dinner, as usual, was not improved by having been ready for an hour. The lamp was not lighted when he came in, and only the cold reflection of the street lights outside, with a parti-coloured gleam at the corner window from his own red and blue professional ensign at the surgery door, lighted the solitary little room, where he looked in vain even for so much as a note or letter to bring some shadow of human fellowship to his home; the fire smouldering dully, the big chair turned with a sullen back against the wall, as if nobody ever sat there—though Nettie had once and forever appropriated it to her use—everything in such inhuman trim and good order disgusted the doctor. He rang his bell violently for the lights and refreshments which were so slow of coming, and, throwing himself into that chair, bit his nails and stared out at the lamplight in the rapid access of thought that came upon him. The first thing that disturbed him in this was the apparition of a figure outside peering in with some anxiety at the blank windows—somebody who was evidently curious to know whether the doctor had yet come home. The unhappy doctor started, and rang his bell once more with furious iteration. He knew what was coming. Somebody else, no doubt, had taken ill, without any consideration for young Rider’s dinner, which, however, a man must manage to swallow even when tormented with importunate patients, and in love. But the knock of the untimely visitor sounded at the much-assailed door before Mary, sulky and resistant, had been able to arrange before the hungry doctor the half-warm half-cold viands which his impatience would not permit to be duly “heated up;” and he had just seated himself to dispose of the unsatisfactory meal when the little groom, who was as tired as his master, opened the door for Mrs. Smith from St. Roque’s. Mrs. Smith was a familiar periodical visitor at Dr. Rider’s. She had not ceased to hold to that hasty and unwise financial arrangement into which the doctor was persuaded to enter when Fred’s pipe had exasperated the landlady into rebellion. He had supplemented the rent at that exciting moment rather than have Nettie disturbed; and now that poor Fred’s pipe was extinguished forever, the doctor still paid the imposition demanded from him—half because he had no time to contest it, half because it was, however improper and unnecessary, a kind of pleasure to do something for Nettie, little as she knew and deeply as she would have resented it. Dr. Rider’s brows cleared up at sight of Nettie’s landlady. He expected some little private anecdotes of her and her ways, such as no one else could give him. He gave Mrs. Smith a chair with a benignity to which she had no personal claim. Her arrival made Dr. Rider’s beefsteak palatable, though the cookery and condition of the same were, to say the least, far from perfect. Mrs. Smith evidently was a little embarrassed with the gracious reception she received. She twisted the corner of her shawl in her fingers as if it had been that apron with which women of her class habitually relieve their feelings. She was in a false position. She came with the worst of news to the melancholy lover, and he treated her as if she brought some special message or favour from the lady of his thoughts.

===Chinese===
没过多久，医生便觉察到了他多舛路上的那个幽灵。在卡林福德（Carlingford），谁在那几条对这位大个子来说总显得过于狭窄的街上遇见这个高大的澳洲丛林人（Bushman），都免不了要对那个野人般的汉子怀几分好奇。莱德医生打从这个陌生人头一回露面起，便带着一种含着醋意的关注注视他，只是直到如今，他同那位高大的陌生人连在一起的念头，只有一个——回澳洲去，而他确信妮蒂（Nettie）决计不肯答应。正是怀着这个念头，他在那天晚上——就是他自己掉转车头、只要那几扇红光照亮的窗户还在视线之内，便一路回头张望、寻找始终不肯露面的妮蒂的那个晚上——眼看着那个陌生人朝那座小屋的方向走去。医生得去出诊看一个远地的病人，在卡林福德另一头几英里之外。他沿着回声隆隆的大道疾驰而去，一路上尽有工夫想象：妮蒂此刻也许正受人纠缠、受人作践；等他看过病人、尽了职分——马车里点起了灯，凛冽的寒风刀子似的刮在脸上，远处卡林福德的灯火在前方鼓舞着他，正又一次往回赶的时候——一种同妮蒂颇为相近的焦躁，蓦地攫住了医生。相近，却又有分别；因为在他身上，那是一种感官的冲动，一种令人心醉的速度与奔行的力气在他心里激起的灵感——他就带着这股劲，冲进那令人振作的冷空气里，自己是自己的主人，马是他的，眼前那条绵延的僻路也是他的。莱德医生又一次起了念头：径直冲到圣罗克小屋（St. Roque’s）去，把妮蒂抢走，管它谁肯反对。他在黑暗里一路疾驰，这念头叫他得意；正是它的不可能，使这幅图景愈发甜美。一下子把她夺走，轰轰烈烈地向一切境遇挑战，赢得幸福与爱情，管它随后是什么。在那一刻的血热里，医生忽然问自己：这毕竟是不是最聪明的法子？无论后头有什么，幸福难道不值得迎头撞上背后那一整列黑压压的麻烦？而且，用一个突然的了断把一切逼到眼前，岂不正是解开这团乱麻的最好路数？他还沉在这种心绪里，便到了自己的家门口；那里的晚饭，照例因为早早备好了个把钟头，愈发不可下咽了。他进来时灯还没有点，只有外头街灯的冷光，加上诊疗室门口他那盏红蓝两色的职业徽灯投在角窗上的一片杂色光亮，照着这间冷清清的小屋子；他想在这里寻到哪怕一张字条、一封信，好给这个家带来一点人间相伴的影子，也是枉然；炉火闷闷地阴燃着，那把大椅子板着一张愠怒的脊背朝墙转了过去，仿佛从来没有人坐过——虽说妮蒂早已一劳永逸地把它占作了己用——一切都收拾得那样没有人气、那样齐整，叫医生满心生厌。他狠狠地打铃，要那迟迟不来的灯和茶点；随后一头陷进那把椅子，咬着指甲，在骤然涌上心头的疾驰的思绪里，直勾勾地望着外头的灯光。这思绪里头一件搅扰他的，是外头一个人影的显现——那人影带着几分焦灼，凑在黑洞洞的窗前朝里窥看，分明是急于知道医生回来了没有。那位不快的医生吓了一跳，又气冲冲地连连打起铃来。他知道要来的是什么。不用说，又有人病倒了，全不顾小莱德的晚饭；可是一个男人，即便叫纠缠不休的病人折磨着，又坠在情网里，这顿饭也总得设法咽下去。可是，不合时宜的来客的敲门声，已经在那扇饱受敲击的门上响了起来，那时候玛丽（Mary）还正绷着脸、一肚子不情愿，没能赶在饥肠辘辘的医生面前摆好那几样半温半凉的菜肴——医生的不耐烦本不许人好好去“回锅加热”；他刚刚坐下，正要对付这顿不称心的饭食，那个同主人一样累乏的小马夫，便替圣罗克小屋来的史密斯太太（Mrs. Smith）开了门。史密斯太太是莱德医生家一位熟识的、按期而来的访客。当初弗雷德（Fred）的烟斗把女房东激得反了抗，医生被人劝着订下了那桩匆忙而欠明智的钱财安排；她至今没有放弃过它。在那个激荡的时刻，他宁可自己贴补房租，也不肯让妮蒂受惊扰；如今可怜的弗雷德的烟斗已经永远熄灭了，医生却还在照付那笔强加给他的钱——一半因为他没有工夫去争竞，一半因为，这事情虽然既不妥当又不必要，能为妮蒂做一点事，终归是一种快乐，尽管她所知甚少，而且一旦知道，必定深恶痛绝。一见了妮蒂的女房东，莱德医生的眉头便开朗了。他指望听到一点关于她本人、她的行止的小小私房轶事，那是旁的谁也给不了他的。他请史密斯太太坐下，那副和蔼劲儿，本不是她个人所敢企望的。她的到来，叫莱德医生那块牛排变得可口起来，虽说就其烹调与成色而论，说得客气些，也远算不上完善。史密斯太太受了这样优渥的接待，分明有点局促。她用手指捻着她披巾的一角，仿佛捻的是她的围裙——她那个阶层的女人，惯常拿围裙来排解心绪。她的处境很尴尬。她是带着最坏的消息，来见这位郁郁的恋人的；他接待她，倒仿佛她捎来了他心上那位小姐的什么特别口信或恩宠。

===Original===
“Well, Mrs. Smith, and how are you all at the cottage?” said the doctor, applying himself leisurely to his beefsteak.

“Well, doctor, nothing to brag of,” said Mrs. Smith, fixing her eyes upon the fringe of her shawl. “I haven’t nothing to say that’s pleasant, more the pity. I don’ know, sir, how you’ll take it when you come to hear; but it’s come very hard upon me. Not for the sake of the lodgings, as’ll let again fast enough, now the poor gentleman’s sad fate is partly forgotten; but you know, doctor, a body gets attached-like when one set of people stays long enough to feel at home; and there ain’t many young ladies like Miss if you were to search the country through. But, now she’s really give in to it herself, there ain’t no more to be said. I never could bring myself to think Miss would give in till tonight when she told me; though Smith he always said, when the stranger gentleman took to coming so constant, as he knew how it would be.”

“For heaven’s sake, what do you mean?” cried Dr. Rider, pushing away his plate, and rising hurriedly from that dinner which was fated never to be eaten. Mrs. Smith shook her head and drew out her handkerchief.

===Chinese===
“怎么样，史密斯太太，小屋里各位都好？”医生说，慢条斯理地对付起他的牛排来。

“唉，医生，没什么可夸耀的，”史密斯太太说，眼睛盯着她披巾上的流苏。“我没有一句好听的话可说，可怜见的。先生，我不知道您听说了会作何感想；可这事情落到我头上，实在难熬。倒不是为了那几间租屋——如今那位可怜先生的惨祸，人家已经淡忘了一半，房子很快还租得出去；可是您知道，医生，一户人家住得久了，住得有了在家的滋味，人心里便有点依依的了。跑遍全国，也找不出几位像小姐这样的年轻姑娘。可是如今她自己当真认了，那也就没什么可说的了。直到今晚她亲口告诉我，我从前怎么也不能让自己相信小姐会认这个；不过史密斯早就说过，打从那位外乡先生来得那样勤，他就知道事情会怎么收场。”

“老天爷，您这是什么意思？”莱德医生喊道，把盘子一推，从那顿注定永远吃不成的晚饭旁急忙站起身来。史密斯太太摇了摇头，抽出了手帕。

===Original===
“I know nothing more, doctor, but just they’re going off to Australia,” said the landlady, mournfully; “and Miss has started packing the big boxes as have been in the hattic since ever they come: they’re going off back where they come from—that’s all as I know.”

“Impossible!” cried the doctor.

“I’d have said so myself this morning,” said Mrs. Smith; “but there ain’t nothing impossible, doctor, as Miss takes in her head. Don’t you go and rush out after her, Dr. Rider. I beg of you upon my knees, if it was my last word! I said to Smith I’d come up and tell the doctor, that he mightn’t hear from nobody promiscuous as couldn’t explain, and mightn’t come rushing down to the cottage to know the rights of it and find the gentleman there unexpected. If there’s one thing I’m afeard of, it’s a quarrel between gentlemen in my house. So, doctor, for the love of peace, don’t you go anear the cottage. I’ll tell you everything if you listen to me.”

===Chinese===
“别的我什么都不知道，医生，就只知道他们要动身回澳洲去了，”女房东哀伤地说；“小姐已经动手收拾那几只大箱子了——他们打一搬来就搁在阁楼里的那几只：他们要回他们来的地方去了——我知道的就这些。”

“不可能！”医生喊道。

“今儿早上我自己也会这么说，”史密斯太太说；“可是医生，凡是小姐一头扎进去的事，就没有什么不可能。您可别冲出去追她，莱德医生。哪怕这是我最后一句话，我也要跪下来求您！我跟史密斯说好了，我要上来告诉医生一声，省得他从什么讲不清缘由的人嘴里胡乱听了个信儿，又省得他一阵风冲到小屋去问个究竟，冷不防撞见那位先生在那儿。我要是怕一件事，就怕在我屋子里两位先生吵架。所以呀，医生，看在太平的份上，您可别挨近小屋。您听我说，我把什么都讲给您。”

===Original===
The doctor, who had snatched up his hat and made a rapid step towards the door, came back and seized hold of his visitor’s shoulder, all his benignity having been put to flight by her unlooked-for revelation. “Look here! I want the truth, and no gossip! What do you mean—what gentleman? What is it all about?” cried Dr. Rider, hoarse with sudden passion.

“Oh, bless you, doctor, don’t blame it upon me, sir,” cried Mrs. Smith. “It ain’t neither my fault nor my business, but that you’ve always been kind, and my heart warms to Miss. It’s the gentleman from Australia as has come and come again; and being an unmarried gentleman, and Miss—you know what she is, sir—and I ask you, candid, Dr. Rider, what was anybody to suppose?”

===Chinese===
医生早已一把抓起帽子，急步朝门口去了，一听她这个出人意料的揭晓，又转了回来，一把攥住这位访客的肩膀，他满面和蔼，早叫她这番话赶得无影无踪。“你听着！我要真话，不要闲话！你到底是什么意思——什么先生？这究竟是怎么回事？”莱德医生喊道，一阵骤起的激愤，喊哑了嗓子。

“哎哟，老天保佑，医生，可别把这个账算到我头上，先生，”史密斯太太嚷道。“这既不是我的过错，也不是我的事，无非是您向来待人和气，我的心又疼着小姐。是那位从澳洲来的先生，来了一趟又一趟；又是个没成家的先生，又是小姐——她是个什么样的人，先生，您是知道的——我跟您说句公道话，莱德医生，换了谁，能不那样想吗？”

===Original===
The doctor grew wildly red up to his hair. He bit his lips over some furious words which Carlingford would have been horrified to hear, and grasped Mrs. Smith’s shoulder with a closer pressure. “What did she tell you?” said the doctor. “Let me have it word for word. Did she say she was going away?—did she speak of this—this—fellow?” exclaimed the doctor, with an adjective over which charity drops a tear. “Can’t you tell me without any supposes, what did she say?”

“I’m not the woman to stand being shook—let me go this minute, sir,” cried Mrs. Smith. “The Australian gentleman is a very nice-spoken civil man, as was always very respectful to me. She came into my back-parlour, doctor, if you will know so particular—all shining and flashing, like as she does when something’s happened. I don’t make no doubt they had been settling matters, them two, and so I told Smith. ‘Mrs. Smith,’ said Miss, in her hasty way, enough to catch your breath coming all of a sudden, ‘I can’t stand this no longer—I shall have to go away—it ain’t no good resisting.’ Them were her very words, Dr. Rider. ‘Get me out the big boxes, please,’ said Miss. ‘It’s best done quietly. You must take your week’s notice, Mrs. Smith, from this day;’ and with that she kept moving about the room all in a flutter like, not able to rest. ‘Do go and get me out those boxes; there’s always a ship on the 24th,’ she says, taking up my knitting and falling to work at it to keep her hands steady. ‘The day afore Christmas!’ says I; ‘and oh, Miss, it’s running in the face of Providence to sail at this time of the year. You’ll have dreadful weather, as sure as life.’ You should have seen her, doctor! She gave a sort of smile up at me, all flashing as if those eyes of her were the sides of a lantern, and the light bursting out both there and all over. ‘All the better,’ she says, as if she’d have liked to fight the very wind and sea, and have her own way even there. Bless you, she’s dreadful for having her own way. A good easy gentleman now, as didn’t mind much—Dr. Rider—Doctor!—you’re not a-going, after all I’ve told you? Doctor, doctor, I say—”

===Chinese===
医生满脸狂红，一直红到头发根。他把几句狂怒的话咬在唇齿之间——那些话若是叫卡林福德听见，准要吓坏了——一面把史密斯太太的肩膀攥得更紧。“她跟你说了什么？”医生说。“一个字一个字，原原本本说给我听。她说她要走吗？——她提没提起这个——这个——家伙？”医生嚷道，中间夹着一个形容词，连“仁慈”听了也要落泪。“你就不能撇开那些想当然，告诉我她到底说了什么？”

“我可不是一个受得住人家摇撼的女人——这会儿就撒手，先生，”史密斯太太嚷道。“那位澳洲先生是个说话顶和气、客客气气的人，待我一向很恭敬。她走进了我的后客厅，医生，您既然非要问得这样仔细——满身光彩焕发、眼睛闪亮，就跟出了什么事的时候一个样。我毫不怀疑，他们两个是已经把事情议定了，我还这样告诉了史密斯。‘史密斯太太，’小姐说，还是那副急脾气，劈头一句，能叫人一下子喘不上气来，‘这个我再也受不了啦——我只得走了——抗拒是没有用的。’这就是她的原话，莱德医生。‘请把那几只大箱子给我取出来，’小姐说。‘这事顶好悄悄地办。您得收我这个礼拜的辞工知会，史密斯太太，从今天算起；’说着，她就在屋里团团地转，像失了魂似的，一刻也安顿不下来。‘快去把那几只箱子给我搬出来；每逢二十四号总有船开，’她说，一边拿起我的毛线活，织了起来，好叫手稳住。‘圣诞前一日！’我说；‘哎哟，小姐，一年里挑这个时节出海，那可是顶着上天行事啊。您准得碰上可怕的坏天气，拿性命担保。’您真该看看她那样子，医生！她朝我仰起脸来，那么笑了笑，满脸闪亮，仿佛她那两只眼睛就是一盏灯笼的两面，光从那里迸出来，又迸得满身都是。‘那样倒更好，’她说，那口气，就像她恨不得同风浪本身较量一场，连在那上头也要由着自己的性子。老天爷，她那个由着性子，可真是吓人的。倒是位好性儿、随和的先生呢，本也不大计较这些——莱德医生——医生！——听了我告诉您的这些，您这不是要走吗？医生，医生，我说——”

===Original===
But what Mrs. Smith said was inaudible to Edward Rider. The door rang in her ears as he dashed it after him, leaving her mistress of the field. There, where he had once left Nettie, he now, all-forgetful of his usual fastidious dislike of gossip, left Mrs. Smith sole occupant of his most private territories. At this unlooked-for crisis the doctor had neither a word nor a moment to spend on anyone. He rushed out of the house, oblivious of all those professional necessities which limit the comings and goings of a doctor in great practice; he did not even know what he was going to do. Perhaps it was an anxious husband or father whom he all but upset as he came out, with sudden impetuosity, into the unfrequented street; but he did not stop to see. Pale and desperate, he faced the cold wind which rushed up between the blank garden-walls of Grange Lane. At Mr. Wodehouse’s door he stumbled against young Wentworth coming out, and passed him with a muttered exclamation which startled the curate. All the floating momentary jealousies of the past rushed back upon the doctor’s mind as he passed that tall figure in the wintry road: how he had snatched Nettie from the vague kindnesses of the young clergyman—the words he had addressed to her on this very road—the answer she had given him once, which had driven him wild with passion and resentment. Impossible! the Australian, it appeared, had found nothing impossible in those circumstances in which Nettie had intrenched herself. Had the doctor’s wisdom been monstrous folly, and his prudence the blindest shortsightedness? He asked himself the question as he rushed on towards that lighted window shining far along the dark road—the same window which he had seen Nettie’s shadow cross, which had been opened to light poor Fred upon the way he never could tread again. Within that jealous blind, shining in that softened domestic light, what drama, murderous to the doctor’s peace, might be going on now?

===Chinese===
可是史密斯太太还说了些什么，爱德华·莱德（Edward Rider）已经听不见了。他夺门而去，那扇门砰地一声在他身后震响，在她耳边回荡——整个战场留给了她。就在他从前丢下妮蒂的同一个地方，这一回，他又一次——全然忘记了自己素来那种嫌弃闲话的洁癖——把史密斯太太单独撇在了他最隐秘的疆土之上。在这个出人意料的关头，医生对任何人都既没有一句话、也没有一刻钟可花费。他冲出屋去，把一个业务繁盛的医生种种限制出入往来的职业必需忘得一干二净；他甚至不知道自己要去做什么。他猝然闯进那条行人稀少的街上，出门时差点儿被他撞翻的，大约是一位忧心忡忡的丈夫或父亲；可他连停下来看一眼也没有。他面色惨白、不顾死活，迎着从格兰奇巷（Grange Lane）两堵光秃秃的花园墙之间卷上来的寒风走去。在伍德豪斯先生（Mr. Wodehouse）家门口，他同正走出门来的年轻的温特沃斯先生（Mr. Wentworth）撞了个满怀，喉咙里咕哝出一声惊呼便擦身而过，吓了副牧师一跳。当他从那冬日大路上那个颀长的身影旁边走过时，往日种种漂浮不定的、一时一变的妒意，一齐涌回医生的心头：他曾怎样把妮蒂从那位年轻牧师朦胧的好意里夺了过来——他曾在这同一条路上对她说过的那些话——她当年给过他的那个回答，那个曾把他逼得激情与怨愤一齐发狂的回答。不可能！可是看来，在妮蒂给自己构筑的层层壁垒之内，那位澳洲人竟没有发现任何不可能的东西。难道医生的明智竟是骇人的愚妄，他的审慎竟是最盲目的短视？他一面朝那扇远远照亮黑暗道路的亮窗奔去，一面这样问自己——正是那扇他曾望见妮蒂的影子横过的窗，正是那扇曾开向亮光、照着可怜的弗雷德走上那条他再也不能踏上的路的窗。在那道惹人妒忌的百叶帘之内，在那片柔和的家常光亮之中，此刻正上演着怎样的、对医生的安宁不啻谋杀的一幕呢？

### XVI / 十六

===Original===

Nettie had taken her resolution all at once. Breathless in sudden conviction, angry, heated, yet seeing in the midst of her excitement no help but an immediate action, the hasty little woman had darted into the heart of the difficulty at once. Every moment she lingered wore her out and disgusted her more with the life and fate which, nevertheless, it was impossible to abandon or shrink from. Nothing was so safe as to make matters irrevocable﻿—to plunge over the verge at once. All gleaming with resolve and animation﻿—with the frosty, chill, exhilarating air which had kindled the colour in her cheeks and the light in her eyes﻿—with haste, resentment, every feeling that can quicken the heart and make the pulses leap﻿—Nettie had flashed into the little parlour, where all was so quiet and leisurely. There Susan sat in close confabulation with the Bushman. The children had been banished out of the room, because their mother’s head was not equal to their noise and restlessness. When they came in with Nettie, as was inevitable, Mrs. Fred sustained the invasion with fretful looks and a certain peevish abstraction. She was evidently interrupted by the rapid entrance, which was as unwelcome as it was hasty. Cold though the night was, Mrs. Fred, leaning back upon her sofa, fanned her pink cheeks with her handkerchief, and looked annoyed as well as disturbed when her children came trooping into the room clamorous for tea behind the little impetuous figure which at once hushed and protected them. Susan became silent all at once, sank back on the sofa, and concealed the faded flush upon her cheeks and the embarrassed conscious air she wore behind the handkerchief which she used so assiduously. Neither she nor her visitor took much share in the conversation that rose round the domestic table. Nettie, too, was sufficiently absorbed in her own concerns to say little, and nobody there was sufficiently observant to remark what a sudden breath of haste and nervous decision inspired the little household ruler as she dispensed the family bread-and-butter. When tea was over, Nettie sent her children out of the way with peremptory distinctness, and stayed behind them to make her communication. If she noticed vaguely a certain confused impatience and desire to get rid of her in the looks of her sister and the Australian, she attached no distinct meaning to it, but spoke out with all the simplicity of an independent power, knowing all authority and executive force to lie in her own hands alone.

===Chinese===

妮蒂一下子就拿定了主意。她被突如其来的信念逼得喘不过气，又气又急，然而在满心激荡之中，她看到的唯一出路就是立刻行动；这个急躁的小女人一下子就扑进了难题的心脏里。她多耽搁一刻，便多消耗一分，便对那种生活、那种命运多生一分憎恶——尽管那种生活与命运，她根本不可能抛下，也不可能躲开。最稳妥的莫过于把事情弄得无可挽回——一头栽过崖边去。她浑身闪着决心的光、精神的光——那严寒清冽、令人振作的冷空气早已点燃了她双颊的红晕与眼里的光亮——带着急切、带着怨愤，带着一切能使心跳加速、脉搏奔涌的情感——妮蒂一阵风似的闪进了那间小客厅，那里的一切原是那样安静、那样从容。苏珊（Susan）正坐在那里，同澳洲丛林人（Bushman）密谈。孩子们都被撵出了屋子，因为他们母亲那颗脑子实在应付不了他们的吵闹与躁动。他们跟着妮蒂（Nettie）进屋来——这是免不了的——弗雷德太太（Mrs. Fred）便用烦躁的眼色和一种别扭的心不在焉，抵挡了这场入侵。这次疾风似的进门分明打搅了她，既不受欢迎，又来得冒失。夜虽冷，弗雷德太太却仰靠在沙发上，用手帕扇着她粉红的脸颊；当她的孩子们簇拥着那个小小的、风风火火的身影——那身影既喝住了他们，又护着他们——吵吵嚷嚷地涌进屋里来要茶喝的时候，她一副又恼又乱的神气。苏珊蓦地不作声了，朝沙发里一靠，用手帕遮住脸上褪去的红晕和她那副窘迫而心虚的神气——那方手帕，她用得那样勤。围着家里那张饭桌升起的谈话，她同她的客人都没有多插嘴。妮蒂也一心想着自己的事，话很少；那里也没有谁留心到，这位小小的家政权柄执掌者在分派全家的面包黄油时，浑身上下透着一股何等急迫的气息、何等紧张的决断。茶用过了，妮蒂用断然分明的口气把她的孩子们打发到一边去，自己留在后头，要说她的事。她若是从姐姐和那位澳洲人的眼色里隐约看出一点乱糟糟的不耐烦、一点想打发她走的意思，她也没有给它什么明确的解释，只以一个独立权力的全部坦率把话直说了出来，因为她知道，一切的权威与执行的权柄，都单单握在她自己一个人手里。

===Original===

“When do you think you can be ready to start? My mind is made up. I shall set to work immediately to prepare,” said Nettie. “Now, look here, Susan: you have been thinking of it for months, so it is not like taking you by surprise. There is a ship that sails on the 24th. If everything is packed and ready, will you consent to go on that day?”

Mrs. Fred started with unfeigned surprise, and, not without a little consternation, turned her eyes towards her friend before answering her sister. “It is just Nettie’s way,” cried Susan﻿—“just how she always does﻿—holds out against you to the very last, and then turns round and darts off before you can draw your breath. The 24th! and this is the 19th! Of course we can’t do it, Nettie. I shall want quantities of things, and Mr. Chatham, you know, is not used to your ways, and can’t be whisked off in a moment whenever you please.”

“I daresay it’s very kind of Mr. Chatham,” said Nettie; “but I can take you out very well by myself﻿—just as well as I brought you here. And I can’t afford to get you quantities of things, Susan. So please to understand I am going off to pack up, and on the 24th we shall go.”

===Chinese===

“你们想想，什么时候能收拾停当、可以动身？我主意已经打定。我马上就动手去准备，”妮蒂说。“听着，苏珊：这件事你心里盘算了好几个月了，所以并不算出其不意地难为你。有一条船，二十四号开。要是全都装好了箱、收拾停当了，你们肯不肯就在那天动身？”

弗雷德太太当真吓了一跳，眼睛先朝她的朋友转了过去，不免还带着几分惶惑，然后才回答她的妹妹。“妮蒂就是这脾气，”苏珊喊道——“她从来就是这样——跟你顶到最后一刻，然后一个转身，不等你喘过一口气就飞走了。二十四号！今天已经是十九号了！当然办不到，妮蒂。我得置办许许多多的东西；还有查塔姆先生（Mr. Chatham），你知道，他过不惯你那套章法，不能你高兴什么时候，就一下子把他卷了走。”

“我想查塔姆先生这份心意是很好的，”妮蒂说；“可我一个人带你们出去，一样带得很好——同当初我把你们带到这儿来的时候一模一样。而且我也置办不起许许多多的东西，苏珊。所以请你们明白：我这就去收拾行装，二十四号我们就动身。”

===Original===

Once more, under Nettie’s impatient eyes, a look and a smile passed between her sister and the Australian. Never very patient at any time, the girl was entirely aggravated out of all toleration now.

“I can’t tell what you may have to smile to each other about,” said Nettie. “It is no very smiling business to me. But since I am driven to it, I shall go at once or not at all. And so that you understand me, that is all I want to say.”

===Chinese===

在妮蒂不耐烦的目光底下，她姐姐同那位澳洲人之间，又一次递过一个眼色、一丝微笑。这位姑娘素来就没什么耐性，这会儿更是一下子被激得全然忍无可忍了。

“我不知道你们两个彼此有什么可笑的，”妮蒂说。“这件事在我可没什么可笑。可我既然是被逼到这一步，我就要么立刻走，要么索性不走。就这样，只要你们明白我的意思，我要说的就全说完了。”

===Original===

With which words she disappeared suddenly to the multitudinous work that lay before her, thinking as little of Susan’s opposition as of the clamour raised by the children, when the hard sentence of going half an hour earlier to bed was pronounced upon them. Nettie’s haste and peremptoriness were mixed, if it must be told, with a little resentment against the world in general. She had ceased being sad﻿—she was roused and indignant. By the time she had subdued the refractory children, and disposed of them for the night, those vast Australian boxes, which they had brought with them across the seas, were placed in the little hall, under the pale light of the lamp, ready for the process of packing, into which Nettie plunged without a moment’s interval. While Mrs. Smith told Edward Rider her story, Nettie was flying up and down stairs with armfuls of things to be packed, and pressing Smith himself into her service. Ere long the hall was piled with heaps of personal property, ready to be transferred to those big receptacles. In the excitement of the work her spirit rose. The headlong haste with which she carried on her operations kept her mind in balance. Once or twice Susan peeped out from the parlour door, and something like an echo of laughter rang out into the hall after one of those inspections. Nettie took no notice either of the look or the laugh. She built in those piles of baggage with the rapidest symmetrical arrangement, to the admiration of Smith, who stood wondering by, and did what he could to help her, with troubled good-nature. She did not stop to make any sentimental reflections, or to think of the thankless office in which she was about to confirm herself beyond remedy by this sudden and precipitate step. Thinking had done Nettie little good hitherto. She felt herself on her true ground again, when she took to doing instead. The lamp burned dimly overhead, throwing down a light confused with frost upon the hall, all encumbered with the goods of the wandering family. Perhaps it was with a certain unconscious symbolism that Nettie buried her own personal wardrobe deep in the lowest depths, making that the foundation for all the after superstructure. Smith stood by, ready to hand her anything she might want, gazing at her with doubtful amazement. The idea of setting off to Australia at a few days’ notice filled him with respect and admiration.

===Chinese===

说完这些话，她蓦地不见了，去对付那摆在她面前的一大堆活计；苏珊的反对，同孩子们被宣判提前半个钟头上床时掀起的那片鼓噪，在她心里一样没有分量。说句实话，妮蒂的急切与专断里，还掺着一点对整个世界的小小怨气。她不再悲伤了——她振作起来，满心愤懑。等她镇服了那几个不听话的孩子、把他们安顿睡下，那几只他们漂洋过海运来的大澳洲箱子，已经摆在那间小门厅里，在一盏昏灯惨淡的光底下，只等装箱了；妮蒂一刻不停，一头扎了进去。当史密斯太太（Mrs. Smith）把她的故事讲给爱德华·莱德（Edward Rider）听的时候，妮蒂正抱着一怀一怀待装的物件，楼上楼下地飞跑，还把史密斯（Smith）本人也拉来给她使唤。不多时，门厅里已经堆起一堆堆的随身物件，只等搬进那些大箱子去。干活的兴奋叫她的精神高涨起来。那种一头冲在前的急促劲儿，倒把她的心思稳在了平衡上。苏珊有一两次从客厅门口张望出来，其中一次张望之后，门厅里响起一声近似回声的笑声。妮蒂对那眼色也罢、那笑声也罢，一概不加理会。她以最迅疾、最匀称的章法，把那些成堆的行李一件件砌筑进去，看得史密斯在旁边直发怔，怀着不安的好心肠尽力给她搭手。她没有停下来作什么多愁善感的省察，也没有去想，这一步急促鲁莽的突然之举，就要把她无可挽回地钉在一个吃力不讨好的职分上。迄今为止，思考给妮蒂带来的好处不多。她一改而去做，便觉得自己重新踏上了真正的立足之地。头顶的灯燃得昏昏的，把一片同寒霜搅浑了的清光洒在门厅里，满地壅塞着这个漂泊之家的一应家当。妮蒂把她自己的衣物深深埋进最底下的一层，拿它给后来整个的上层建筑打基脚——这中间也许带着一种她自己也不自知的象征意味。史密斯站在旁边，随时预备递上她可能要的任何东西，带着将信将疑的惊愕凝望着她。几天之内说走就走、动身去澳洲的这个念头，叫他满心敬服、赞叹不已。

===Original===

“A matter of a three months’ voyage,” said Smith; “and if I might make bold to ask, Miss, if the weather ain’t too bad for anything, how will you pass away the time on board ship when there ain’t nobody to speak to?﻿—but, to be sure, the gentleman﻿—”

“The gentleman is not going with us,” said Nettie, peremptorily﻿—“and there are the children to pass away the time. My time passes too quick, whatever other people’s may do. Where is Mrs. Smith, that I see nothing of her tonight? Gone out!﻿—how very odd she should go out now, of all times in the world. Where has she gone, do you suppose? Not to be ungrateful to you, who are very kind, a woman is, of course, twenty times the use a man is, in most things. Thank you﻿—not that; those coloured frocks now﻿—there! that bundle with the pink and the blue. One would suppose that even a man might know coloured frocks when he saw them,” said Nettie, with despairing resignation, springing up from her knees to seize what she wanted. “Thank you﻿—I think, perhaps, if you would just go and make yourself comfortable, and read your paper, I should get on better. I am not used to having anybody to help me. I got on quite as well, thank you, by myself.”

===Chinese===

“这可是足足三个月的海路，”史密斯说；“小姐，恕我斗胆问一句，要是天气还不至于坏到什么也做不成，船上又没有人同您说话，您可怎么打发那段日子？——不过，说起来，那位先生——”

“那位先生不跟我们一起去，”妮蒂断然说——“况且还有孩子们，也正好打发日子。我的日子，且不管别人的怎样，总过得太快了。史密斯太太上哪儿去了，今晚我怎么一直没见着她？出门去了！——偏偏挑天底下这个时辰出门，真是怪透了。你猜她上哪儿去了？倒不是不领你的情，你人很好，只是在大多数事情上，一个女人抵得上男人二十倍的用场。谢谢——不是那个；是那些花布衣裳——喏！那个粉的蓝的混在一块儿的包儿。照这么说来，只怕连一个男人见了花布衣裳也认不出来，”妮蒂说，带着绝望的听天由命，从膝头一跃而起，一把抓过她要的东西。“谢谢——我想，要是你肯去舒舒服服待着，看看你的报纸，我说不定还顺当些。我不惯有人帮手。我一个人干，照样干得不坏，谢谢你。”

===Original===

Smith withdrew, not without some confusion and discomfort, to his condemned cell, and Nettie went on silent and swift with her labours. “Quite as well! better!” said Nettie to herself. “Other people never will understand. Now, I know better than to try anybody.” If that hasty breath was a sigh, there was little sound of sorrow in it. It was a little gust of impatience, indignation, intolerance even, and hasty self-assertion. She alone knew what she could do, and must do. Not one other soul in the world beside could enter into her inevitable work and way.

===Chinese===

史密斯退了出去，回到他那间死囚牢似的屋里去，多少带着几分狼狈和不是滋味；妮蒂则不声不响、手脚飞快地继续她的活计。“干得不坏！还要好！”妮蒂暗自说。“旁人永远不会懂。往后我算学乖了，谁也不去指望。”倘若那声急促的吐纳是一声叹息，里头也听不出多少悲音。那是一小阵急躁的旋风——是不耐烦，是愤懑，甚至是难以容忍，还有仓促的自命。唯有她一个人知道她能做什么、必须做什么。普天之下再没有另一个灵魂，进得了她那不可避免的活计与道路。

===Original===

Nettie did not hear the footstep which she might have recognised ringing rapidly down the frosty road. She was too busy rustling about with perpetual motion, folding and refolding, and smoothing into miraculous compactness all the heterogeneous elements of that mass. When a sudden knock came to the door she started, struck with alarm, then paused a moment, looking round her, and perceiving at one hasty glance that nobody could possibly enter without seeing both herself and her occupation, made one prompt step to the door, which nobody appeared to open. It was Mrs. Smith, no doubt; but the sudden breathless flutter which came upon Nettie cast doubts upon that rapid conclusion. She opened it quickly, with a certain breathless, sudden promptitude, and looked out pale and dauntless, understanding by instinct that some new trial to her fortitude was there. On the other hand, Edward Rider pressed in suddenly, almost without perceiving it was Nettie. They were both standing in the hall together, before they fully recognised each other. Then the doctor, gazing round him at the unusual confusion, gave an involuntary groan out of the depths of his heart. “Then it is true!” said Dr. Rider. He stood among the chaos, and saw all his own dreams broken up and shattered in pieces. Even passion failed him in that first bitterness of conviction. Nettie stood opposite, with the sleeves of her black dress turned up from her little white nimble wrists, her hair pushed back from her cheeks, pushed quite behind one delicate ear, her eyes shining with all those lights of energy and purpose which came to them as soon as she took up her own character again. She met his eye with a little air of defiance, involuntary, and almost unconscious. “It is quite true,” said Nettie, bursting forth in sudden self-justification; “I have my work to do, and I must do it as best I can. I cannot keep considering you all, and losing my life. I must do what God has given me to do, or I must die.”

===Chinese===

那阵脚步声，她本可以认出来的——它正沿着结霜的大路一路迅疾地响过来——妮蒂却没有听见。她正忙着窸窸窣窣、转个不停，折了又折，把那一大堆形形色色的东西抚平成奇迹似的紧凑。忽然一阵敲门声传来，她吓了一跳，心头一紧，随即顿了一顿，朝四下里看了看，急促的一瞥便叫她看出：无论谁进来，都不可能不看见她本人和她的活计；于是她朝门口利落地迈出一步——门外却不见有人开门。不用说，是史密斯太太；可是蓦地袭上妮蒂心头的那阵透不过气的慌乱，叫她对这迅速下的断语也疑心起来。她带着一种屏着气的突然的敏捷，飞快把门打开，面色苍白、毫无惧色地朝外望去，本能地觉出：她的刚毅在这里又要受一次新的考验了。另一边，爱德华·莱德突然挤进门来，几乎不曾察觉站在那里的是妮蒂。两个人一起站在门厅里了，才彼此认了出来。随后，医生环顾着这番异乎寻常的乱象，打心底里不由自主地呻吟了一声。“这么说，是真的了！”莱德医生说。他站在这一片纷乱之中，眼看着自己的梦全都碎了，碎成了片片。就在这最初一阵认清事实的苦痛里，连激情也抛下了他。妮蒂站在对面，黑裙子的袖口从她那小小的、白白的、灵巧的手腕上卷起，头发从两颊拢起，完全拢到一只秀气的耳朵后面，她的眼睛闪着光——她一重新拾起自己的本相，那些精力与意志的光亮便一齐来到她的眼里。她迎着他的目光，带着一点挑战的神气——并非有意，几乎全出于无心。“完全是真的，”妮蒂说，骤然迸出满腔自辩；“我有我的活儿要做，我必须尽力做去。我不能老是顾虑你们大家，把我的命搭进去。我必须做上帝交给我做的事，不然我就只有死。”

===Original===

Never had Nettie been so near breaking down, and falling into sudden womanish tears and despair. She would not yield to the overpowering momentary passion. She clutched at the bundle of frocks again, and made room for them spasmodically in the box which she had already packed. Edward Rider stood silent, gazing at her as in her sudden anguish Nettie pulled down and reconstructed that curious honeycomb. But he had not come here merely to gaze, while the catastrophe was preparing. He went up and seized her busy hands, raised her up in spite of her resistance, and thrust away, with an exclamation of disgust, that great box in which all his hopes were being packed away. “There is first a question to settle between you and me,” cried the doctor: “you shall not do it. No; I forbid it, Nettie. Because you are wilful,” cried Edward Rider, hoarse and violent, grasping the hands tighter, with a strain in which other passions than love mingled, “am I to give up all the rights of a man? You are going away without even giving me just warning﻿—without a word, without a sign; and you think I will permit it, Nettie? Never﻿—by heaven!”

“Dr. Edward,” said Nettie, trembling, half with terror, half with resolution, “you have no authority over me. We are two people﻿—we are not one. I should not have gone away without a word or a sign. I should have said goodbye to you, whatever had happened; but that is different from permitting or forbidding. Let us say goodbye now and get it over, if that will please you better,” she cried, drawing her hands from his grasp; “but I do not interfere with your business, and I must do mine my own way.”

===Chinese===

妮蒂从来不曾这样接近崩溃，接近突然堕入女人式的眼泪与绝望。她不肯向那压倒一切的、一时的激情屈服。她重新抓起那包衣裳，痉挛似的在已经装满的箱子里给它们腾出地方。爱德华·莱德默然立着，凝望着她，看她在一阵骤然的剧痛里把那座奇异的蜂巢拆了又重砌。可他到此地来，并不是为了在大祸酝酿的当儿干瞪眼的。他走上前去，抓住她那双忙个不停的手，不顾她的抗拒把她拉起来，又厌恶地喊了一声，把那只正把他的全部希望装进去的大箱子猛地推开。“先有一桩事，得在你我之间了断，”医生喊道：“这事你不许做。不；我不许你，妮蒂。就因为你任性，”爱德华·莱德喊道，声音喑哑而激烈，把手攥得更紧，这一攥里掺着的，不止是爱情，“难道我就得放弃一个男人全部的权利？你连个正当的招呼都不打就要走了——没有一个字，没有一个记号；你还以为我会容许，妮蒂？永不——我对天发誓！”

“爱德华医生，”妮蒂说，发着抖，一半是害怕，一半是决心，“你对我没有权威。我们是两个人——不是一个人。我不该一个字不留、一个记号不留就走掉。无论出了什么事，我总该同你道一声再会；可这同容许或禁止是两回事。要是那样你更好受些，咱们现在就把再会说过了、了结了吧，”她喊道，把手从他手里抽出来；“我不干涉你的事，我的事也必须由我自己做主。”

===Original===

The doctor was in no mood to argue. He thrust the big box she had packed away into a corner, and closed it with a vindictive clang. It gave him a little room to move in that little commonplace hall, with its dim lamp, which had witnessed so many of the most memorable scenes of his life. “Look here,” cried Dr. Rider; “authority has little to do with it. If you had been my wife, Nettie, to be sure you could not have deserted me. It is as great a cruelty﻿—it is as hard upon me, this you are trying to do. I have submitted hitherto, and heaven knows it has been bitter enough; and you scorn me for my submission,” said the doctor, making the discovery by instinct. “When a fellow obeys you, it is only contempt you feel for him; but I tell you, Nettie, I will bear it no longer. You shall not go away. This is not to be. I will neither say goodbye, nor think of it. What is your business is my business; and I declare to you, you shall not go unless I go too. Ah﻿—I forgot. They tell me there is a fellow, an Australian, who ventures to pretend﻿—I don’t mean to say I believe it. You think *he* will not object to your burdens! Nettie! Don’t let us kill each other. Let us take all the world on our shoulders,” cried the doctor, drawing near again, with passionate looks, “rather than part!”

===Chinese===

医生没有心思争辩。他把那只她已装好的大箱子猛推进一个角落，带着泄愤的铿然一响，把它扣上了。这一下，在那间小小的、平平常常的门厅里——那盏昏灯曾照见他一生多少最难忘的场面——他总算有了点转身的余地。“你听着，”莱德医生（Dr. Rider）喊道；“这跟权威不大相干。你若做了我的妻子，妮蒂，你自然不能丢下我不管。可你如今要做的这件事，是同样狠毒的残忍——对我同样是重的折磨。我到今天一直顺从着，天知道这已经苦到十分；而你却因为我顺从，瞧不起我，”医生说，凭本能自己发现了这一点。“一个人只要听你的话，你心里对他便只剩了轻蔑；可是我告诉你，妮蒂，这个我再也忍受不了了。你不许走。这件事不能这样算。再会我不说，连想也不去想它。你的事就是我的事；我向你声明，除非我也同去，否则你不许走。啊——我忘了。他们对我说，有一个人，一个澳洲人，竟敢存那份心思——我并不是说我信了。你以为*他*就不会嫌弃你的重担！妮蒂！咱们别把彼此逼死。咱们宁可把整个世界都扛在自己肩上，”医生喊道，带着炽热的眼神，又凑近前来，“也不要分开！”

===Original===

There was a pause﻿—neither of them could speak at that moment. Nettie, who felt her resolution going, her heart melting, yet knew she dared not give way, clasped her hands tight in each other and stood trembling, yet refusing to tremble; collecting her voice and thoughts. The doctor occupied that moment of suspense in a way which might have looked ludicrous in other circumstances, but was a relief to the passion that possessed him. He dragged the other vast Australian box to the same corner where he had set the first, and piled them one above the other. Then he collected with awkward care all the heaps of garments which lay about, and carried them off in the other direction to the stairs, where he laid them carefully with a clumsy tenderness. When he had swept away all these encumbrances, as by a sudden gust of wind, he came back to Nettie, and once more clasped the firm hands which held each other fast. She broke away from him with a sudden cry﻿—

“You acknowledged it was impossible!” cried Nettie. “It is not my doing, or anybody’s; no one shall take the world on his shoulders for my sake﻿—I ask nobody to bear my burdens. Thank you for not believing it﻿—that is a comfort at least. Never, surely, anyone else﻿—and not you, not you! Dr. Edward, let us make an end of it. I will never consent to put my yoke upon your shoulders, but I﻿—I will never forget you or blame you﻿—any more. It is all hard, but we cannot help it. Goodbye﻿—don’t make it harder, you, who are the only one that﻿—; goodbye﻿—no more﻿—don’t say any more.”

===Chinese===

一阵静默——那一刻谁也说不出话来。妮蒂觉得自己的决心在消融，心在软下去，可她知道她不敢松口，便把两只手紧紧扣在一起，站在那里发抖，又不肯抖；把嗓音和心思都收拢来。医生把那令人悬心的一刻这样占用了；换一种场合，这情形未免显得可笑，可对那攫住他的激情，倒不失为一种排解。他把另一只大澳洲箱子拖到先前那一只搁着的同一个角落，一只摞在另一只上面。随后他带着笨拙的小心，把满地摊着的一堆堆衣物拢在一处，朝另一个方向搬到楼梯口，在那里用一种笨手笨脚的温柔，仔细地放下。等他仿佛凭着一阵突如其来的狂风把这些障碍一齐扫荡干净，他又回到妮蒂跟前，再一次攥住那双紧紧互扣的坚毅的手。她猛地叫出一声，从他手里挣脱了——

“你自己也承认过那是不可能的！”妮蒂喊道。“这不是我的主意，也不是任何人的主意；谁也不许为了我把世界扛在自己肩上——我不求任何人替我担重担。谢谢你没有相信那话——这至少是一点安慰。别人自然一定不会——你更不会，不会的！爱德华医生，咱们把这了结了吧。我决不肯把我的轭搁到你肩上，可是我——我永远不会忘记你，也永远不会再怨你。这一切都苦，可我们没法子。再会——别叫它更苦，你，你是唯一那个——；再会——不要再说——什么也不要再说了。”

===Original===

At this moment the parlour door opened suddenly; Nettie’s trembling mouth and frame, and the wild protest and contradiction which were bursting from the lips of the doctor, were lost upon the spectator absorbed in her own affairs, and full of excitement on her own account, who looked out. “Perhaps Mr. Edward will walk in,” said Mrs. Fred. “Now he is here to witness what I mean, I should like to speak to you, please, Nettie. I did not think I should ever appeal to you, Mr. Edward, against Nettie’s wilfulness﻿—but, really now, we, none of us, can put up with it any longer. Please to walk in and hear what I’ve got to say.”

===Chinese===

正在这时，客厅的门蓦地开了；门口望出来的那位看客一心忙着自己的事，为自己满心激动，对妮蒂颤动的嘴唇和身形、对医生唇边迸出的那阵激烈的抗议与反驳，一概视而不见、听而不闻。“爱德华先生请进来吧，”弗雷德太太说。“他既然在这儿，正好给我要说的话做个见证——那么，请听我说几句，妮蒂。我原以为我决不会求到爱德华先生，请你来对抗妮蒂的任性——可是说真的，到了如今，我们谁也再忍不下去了。请进来吧，听听我要说的话。”

===Original===

The big Bushman stood before the little fire in the parlour, extinguishing its tiny glow with his vast shadow. The lamp burned dimly upon the table. A certain air of confusion was in the room. Perhaps it was because Nettie had already swept her own particular belongings out of that apartment, which once, to the doctor’s eyes, had breathed of her presence in every corner﻿—but it did not look like Nettie’s parlour tonight. Mrs. Fred, with the broad white bands of her cap streaming over her black dress, had just assumed her place on the sofa, which was her domestic throne. Nettie, much startled and taken by surprise, stood by the table, waiting with a certain air of wondering impatience what was to be said to her﻿—with still the sleeves turned up from her tiny wrists, and her fingers unconsciously busy expressing her restless intolerance of this delay by a hundred involuntary tricks and movements. The doctor stood close by her, looking only at Nettie, watching her with eyes intent as if she might suddenly disappear from under his very gaze. As for the Australian, he stood uneasy under Nettie’s rapid investigating glance, and the slower survey which Dr. Rider made on entering. He plucked at his big beard, and spread out his large person with a confusion and embarrassment rather more than merely belonged to the stranger in a family party; while Mrs. Fred, upon her sofa, took up her handkerchief and once more began to fan her pink cheeks. What was coming? After a moment’s pause, upon which Nettie could scarcely keep herself from breaking, Susan spoke.

===Chinese===

那个大个子澳洲丛林人站在客厅里那小小的炉火跟前，把他巨大的影子罩上去，把那点微光遮得一丝不剩。桌上的灯昏昏地燃着。屋里有一股乱糟糟的气氛。也许是因为妮蒂早已把她自己那些私人的物件从这间屋子里一扫而空——这间屋子，在医生眼里，从前每一个角落都透着她在的气息——今晚它看上去，可不像妮蒂的客厅了。弗雷德太太帽子底下那两条宽宽的白带子披拂在黑裙子上，刚刚在她那家常的宝座——沙发——上就了位。妮蒂大大吃了一惊，猝不及防，站在桌旁，带着一种又惊愕又不耐烦的神气，等着听人家要对她说的话——袖口仍旧卷在那小小的手腕上，手指头不知不觉地忙着，用上百种下意识的小动作，表示着她对这番耽搁坐立难安的不能容忍。医生紧挨着她站着，眼睛只看妮蒂，目不转睛地盯着她，仿佛她会从他的眼皮底下蓦然消失似的。至于那位澳洲人，在妮蒂迅疾的、探询的一瞥和莱德医生进门时那较缓慢的打量底下，他站得很不自在。他扯着他那把大胡子，把他那庞大的身躯摊开站着，那份慌乱与窘迫，远远超出了一个家常聚会里的陌生人本应有的分寸；而沙发上的弗雷德太太，拿起她的手帕，又开始扇她那粉红的脸颊了。要来的是什么呢？顿了一顿——那一顿，妮蒂险些儿按捺不住要发作——苏珊开口了。

===Original===

“Nettie has always had the upper hand so much that she thinks I am always to do exactly as she pleases,” burst forth Mrs. Fred; “and I don’t doubt poor Fred encouraged her in it, because he felt he was obliged to my family, and always gave in to her; but now I have somebody to stand by me,” added Susan, fanning still more violently, and with a sound in her voice which betrayed a possibility of tears﻿—“now I have somebody to stand by me﻿—I tell you once for all, Nettie, I will not go on the 24th.”

Nettie gazed at her sister in silence without attempting to say anything. Then she lifted her eyes inquiringly to the Australian, in his uneasy spectator position before the fire. She was not much discomposed, evidently, by that sudden assertion of will﻿—possibly Nettie was used to it﻿—but she looked curious and roused, and rather eager to know what was it now?

“I will not go on the 24th,” cried Mrs. Fred, with a hysterical toss of her head. “I will not be treated like a child, and told to get ready whenever Nettie pleases. She pretends it is all for our sake, but it is for the sake of having her own will, and because she has taken a sudden disgust at something. I asked you in, Mr. Edward, because you are her friend, and because you are the children’s uncle, and ought to know how they are provided for. Mr. Chatham and I,” said Susan, overcome by her feelings, and agitating the handkerchief violently, “have settled﻿—to be﻿—married first before we set out.”

===Chinese===

“妮蒂一向占足了上风，便以为我想怎么样就得全依着她，”弗雷德太太迸出来说；“我不怀疑，可怜的弗雷德（Fred）也是惯纵了她，因为他觉得自己欠着我娘家的情，对她一向让着；可是如今有人给我撑腰了，”苏珊又添上一句，扇得愈发用力，嗓音里有一种声音，泄露了眼泪的可能——“如今有人给我撑腰了——我把话对你说明白了，妮蒂，二十四号我不走。”

妮蒂默然凝视着她的姐姐，什么也没有想说。随后她带着询问的神气，抬起眼睛，望向炉前那位站在不安的旁观位置上的澳洲人。对于那突如其来的意志的宣示，她分明并没有多少慌乱——妮蒂也许是惯了的——可她显得又好奇又振作，很急于知道，如今这又是什么名堂？

“二十四号我不走，”弗雷德太太喊道，歇斯底里地把头一扬。“我不愿让人家当小孩子看待，妮蒂什么时候高兴，就叫我什么时候收拾。她假称这全是为了我们，其实是为了遂她自己的心愿，是因为她忽然对什么事情起了反感。我请你进来，爱德华先生，因为你是她的朋友，又因为你是孩子们的叔叔，应当知道他们往后是怎么安排的。查塔姆先生同我，”苏珊说，感情涌了上来，手帕剧烈地挥动着，“已经议定——先——成了亲，然后再动身。”

===Original===

If a shell had fallen in the peaceful apartment, the effect could not have been more startling. The two who had been called in to receive that intimation, and who up to this moment had been standing together listening languidly enough, too much absorbed in the matter between themselves to be very deeply concerned about anything Mrs. Fred could say or do, fell suddenly apart with the wildest amazement in their looks. “Susan, you are mad!” cried Nettie, gazing aghast at her sister, with an air of mingled astonishment and incredulity. The doctor, too much excited to receive with ordinary decorum information so important, made a sudden step up to the big embarrassed Australian, who stood before the fire gazing into vacancy, and looking the very embodiment of conscious awkwardness. Dr. Rider stretched out both his hands and grasped the gigantic fist of the Bushman with an effusion which took that worthy altogether by surprise. “My dear fellow, I wish you joy﻿—I wish you joy. Anything I can be of use to you in, command me!” cried the doctor, with a suppressed shout of half-incredulous triumph. Then he returned restlessly towards Nettie﻿—they all turned to her with instinctive curiosity. Never in all her troubles had Nettie been so pale; she looked in her sister’s face with a kind of despair.

===Chinese===

哪怕是一颗炮弹落进这太平的屋子里，也不见得比这更叫人吃惊。被叫进来领受这个消息的那两位，直到此刻还站在一起懒洋洋地听着，心思全在他们两人自己的事情上，对弗雷德太太能说什么、能做什么本来并不深切关心——这时蓦地彼此分开，脸上带着天大的惊愕。“苏珊，你疯了！”妮蒂喊道，惊骇地凝视着她的姐姐，神情里惊异与不信混在一起。医生激动得过了头，再也拿不出平常的礼数来接待这样重大的消息，突然一步跨到那个局促的大个子澳洲人跟前——那人正站在炉前，望着一片虚空，活像自知笨拙的化身。莱德医生伸出两只手，一把攥住丛林人的巨拳，那股热络劲儿叫这位好人儿整个儿吃了一惊。“我的老兄，恭喜你——恭喜你。凡是我帮得上忙的，尽管吩咐！”医生喊道，压着嗓子发出一声半信半疑的、胜利的欢呼。随后他坐立不安地转回妮蒂这边——大家凭着本能的好奇，一齐转向了她。妮蒂在她一切患难之中，从来不曾这样苍白过；她望着她姐姐的脸，带着一种近乎绝望的神色。

===Original===

“Is this *true*, Susan?” she said, with a sorrowful wonder as different as possible from the doctor’s joyful surprise﻿—“not something said to vex us﻿—really true? And this has been going on, and I knew nothing of it; and all this time you have been urging me to go back to the colony﻿—*me*﻿—as if you had no other thoughts. If you had made up your mind to this, what was the use of driving me desperate?” cried Nettie, in a sudden outburst of that incomprehension which aches in generous hearts. Then she stopped suddenly and looked from her sister, uttering suppressed sobs, and hiding her face in her handkerchief on the sofa, to the Australian before the fire. “What is the good of talking?” said Nettie, with a certain indignant impatient indulgence, coming to an abrupt conclusion. Nobody knew so well as she did how utterly useless it was to remonstrate or complain. She dropt into the nearest chair, and began with hasty tremulous hands to smooth down the cuffs of her black sleeves. In the bitterness of the moment it was not the sudden deliverance, but the heartlessness and domestic treachery that struck Nettie. She, the champion and defender of this helpless family for years﻿—who had given them bread, and served it to them with her own cheerful unwearied hands﻿—who had protected as well as provided for them in her dauntless innocence and youth. When she was thus cast off on the brink of the costliest sacrifice of all, it was not the delightful sensation of freedom which occurred to Nettie. She fell back with a silent pang of injury swelling in her heart, and, all tremulous and hasty, gave her agitated attention to the simple act of smoothing down her sleeves﻿—a simple but symbolical act, which conveyed a world of meaning to the mind of the doctor as he stood watching her. The work she had meant to do was over. Nettie’s occupation was gone. With the next act of the domestic drama she had nothing to do. For the first time in her life utterly vanquished, with silent promptitude she abdicated on the instant. She seemed unable to strike a blow for the leadership thus snatched from her hands. With proud surprise and magnanimity she withdrew, forbearing even the useless reproaches of which she had impatiently asked, “What was the good?” Never abdicated emperor laid aside his robes with more ominous significance, than Nettie, with fingers trembling between haste and agitation, smoothed down round her shapely wrists those turned-up sleeves.

===Chinese===

“这当真是*真的*吗，苏珊？”她说，那悲怆的惊异，同医生欢喜的吃惊判然两样——“不是气我们才说的——当真？这件事一直在进行，我竟然一无所知；这么些时候，你一直在催我回殖民地——催*我*——仿佛你心里再没有别的念头。你既然打定了这个主意，又何必逼得我走投无路？”妮蒂喊道，胸中骤然迸出那种叫宽厚的心隐隐作痛的不解。随后她蓦地打住，目光从沙发上的姐姐——她正压着呜咽，把脸埋进手帕里——移到炉前的澳洲人身上。“说有什么用？”妮蒂说，带着一点愤懑的、不耐烦的宽容，陡然收了话头。劝阻也罢，抱怨也罢，是何等地全然无用——世上没有谁比她更清楚。她一坐坐进最近的一把椅子，用仓促颤抖的双手，抚平她黑色袖子的袖口。在这一刻的苦涩里，浮上妮蒂心头的，不是那突如其来的解脱，而是那份无情，那份家庭里的背叛。她——这个无助之家多年的捍卫者与保护人——给他们面包，还用她自己那双快活的、不知疲倦的手把面包递到他们手里——在她那无畏的天真与青春里，既供养了他们，又庇护了他们。而在这至重牺牲的悬崖边上，她竟这样被人抛下——此刻浮上妮蒂心头的，并不是自由的甜美滋味。她朝椅背上往后一靠，心头默默地胀起一阵受伤的痛楚，浑身发颤、手脚急促，把激荡的心神全灌注到抚平袖口这件简单的事情上——一个简单而含着象征意味的动作；站在一旁望着她的医生，从这个动作里读出了满心的含义。她本来要做的事，完了。妮蒂的职分，没有了。这出家庭戏剧的下一幕，再没有她的事。她生平第一次被彻底击败，当即默然逊位，干脆利落。对于这样从她手里夺去的权柄，她似乎连一击也不肯出手。她带着高傲的惊异与宽宏退了下来，连那些无用的责备也忍住了——她方才还不耐烦地问过：“说有什么用？”被废黜的皇帝卸下他的袍服，从没有比这更不祥的意味了；而妮蒂，手指在急促与激动之间发着颤，绕着她那双秀美的手腕，把那些卷起的袖口抚平了。

===Original===

The doctor’s better genius saved him from driving the indignant Titania desperate at that critical moment by any ill-advised rejoicings; and the sight of Nettie’s agitation so far calmed Dr. Rider that he made the most sober and decorous congratulations to the sister-in-law, whom for the first time he felt grateful to. Perhaps, had he been less absorbed in his own affairs, he could scarcely have failed to remember how, not yet a year ago, the shabby form of Fred lay on that same sofa from which Susan had announced her new prospects; but in this unexampled revolution of affairs no thought of Fred disturbed his brother, whose mind was thoroughly occupied with the sudden tumult of his own hopes. “Oh yes, I hope I shall be happy at last. After all my troubles, I have to look to myself, Mr. Edward; and your poor brother would have been the last to blame me,” sobbed Mrs. Fred, with involuntary self-vindication. Then followed a pause. The change was too sudden and extraordinary, and involved results too deeply important to every individual present, to make words possible. Mrs. Fred, with her face buried in her handkerchief, and Nettie, her whole frame thrilling with mortification and failure, tremulously trying to button her sleeves, and bestowing her whole mind upon that operation, were discouraging interlocutors; and after the doctor and the Bushman had shaken hands, their powers of communication were exhausted. The silence was at length broken by the Australian, who, clearing his voice between every three words, delivered his embarrassed sentiments as follows:﻿—

===Chinese===

医生身上那位较好的守护神救了他：在这紧要关头，他没有用任何欠斟酌的道贺，把满腔义愤的泰坦妮亚（Titania）逼上绝路；而妮蒂那副激动的情形，也把莱德医生镇住了，叫他向这位姻亲道贺时，端端正正、合乎礼度到了极点——他头一回对这位嫂嫂觉出了感激。也许，他若不是那样一心沉在自己的事里，他几乎不可能不记起：还不满一年之前，弗雷德那副潦倒的身形，恰恰就躺在苏珊宣布她新前程的那同一张沙发上；可是，在这场史无前例的事变里，没有一点对弗雷德的想念搅扰这位做兄弟的，他的心思全被他自己希望的突然骚动占满了。“哦，是的，我希望我到底能幸福了。受够了这些磨难，如今我只得自己顾自己了，爱德华先生；你那可怜的哥哥，也决计最不会怪我，”弗雷德太太啜泣着说，不由自主地替自己辩白。随后是一阵静默。这个变化太突然、太出奇，牵涉到的后果对在场的每一个人又太深切、太重大，叫人说不出话来。脸埋在手帕里的弗雷德太太，和浑身因屈辱与失败而战栗、哆哆嗦嗦地想扣上袖口、把全部心神都贯注在那上面的妮蒂，都是叫人提不起话头的交谈者；医生同丛林人握过了手，他们互通款曲的本钱也就用尽了。那静默终于叫澳洲人打破了；他每说三个字便清一清嗓子，把他那番窘迫的心曲，这样道来：——

===Original===

“I trust, Miss Nettie, you’ll not think you’ve been unfairly dealt by, or that any change is necessary so far as you are concerned. Of course,” said Mr. Chatham, growing red, and plucking at his beard, “neither your sister nor I﻿—found out﻿—till quite lately﻿—how things were going to be; and as for you making any change in consequence, or thinking we could be anything but glad to have you with us﻿—”

Here the alarming countenance of Nettie, who had left off buttoning her sleeves, brought her new relation to a sudden stop. Under the blaze of her inquiring eyes the Bushman could go no farther. He looked at Susan for assistance, but Susan was still absorbed in her handkerchief; and while he paused for expression, the little abdicated monarch took up the broken thread.

===Chinese===

“我相信，妮蒂小姐，您不会认为自己受了不公平的待遇，也不会认为，就您这方面说，有什么需要改变的。自然，”查塔姆先生说，红起了脸，扯着他的胡子，“您姐姐和我，都是直到最近才——看出——事情会变成怎样；至于说您因此要作什么改变，或是以为我们除了高兴有您同我们在一起，还会存别的念头——”

这里，妮蒂停下了扣袖口的手，露出那副叫人胆寒的面色，把她这位新姻亲的话头猛地截住了。在她那双询问的眼睛的逼视之下，丛林人再说不下去了。他朝苏珊望过去求援，可是苏珊还沉迷在她的手帕里；趁他搜索词句的当儿，这位小小的逊位君主，把那根断了的线接上了。

===Original===

“Thank you,” said Nettie, rising suddenly; “I knew you were honest. It is very good of you, too, to be glad to have me with you. You don’t know any better. I’m abdicated, Mr. Chatham; but because it’s rather startling to have one’s business taken out of one’s hands like this, it will be very kind of everybody not to say anything more tonight. I don’t quite understand it all just at this moment. Good night, Dr. Edward. We can talk tomorrow, please; not tonight. You surely understand me, don’t you? When one’s life is changed all in a moment, one does not exactly see where one is standing just at once. Good night. I mean what I say,” she continued, holding her head high with restrained excitement, and trying to conceal the nervous agitation which possessed her as the doctor hastened before her to open the door. “Don’t come after me, please; don’t say anything; I cannot bear any more tonight.”

===Chinese===

“谢谢你，”妮蒂说，蓦地站起身来；“我知道你为人诚实。你高兴有我同你们在一起，这心肠也很好。你不懂得还有别的道理。我是逊了位了，查塔姆先生；不过，一个人的事务，这样一下子叫人从手里拿了过去，总不免叫人心惊，所以今晚请各位什么也不要再说了，那才是厚道。眼下这一刻，这一切我还没有全弄明白。晚安，爱德华医生。我们明天再谈吧，求你；今晚不谈。你自然明白我的意思，是不是？一个人的整个生活一朝变了样，他不会立刻就看清自己站在哪里。晚安。我说的是认真的，”她接着说，抑制着激动，把头昂得高高的，竭力掩饰那攫住她的神经的震颤——医生已经抢在她前头去开门了。“请别跟我出来；什么也不要说；今晚我再也受不住了。”

===Original===

“But tomorrow,” said the doctor, holding fast the trembling hand. Nettie was too much overstrained and excited to speak more. A single sudden sob burst from her as she drew her hand out of his, and disappeared like a flying sprite. The doctor saw the heaving of her breast, the height of self-restraint which could go no further. He went back into the parlour like a true lover, and spied no more upon Nettie’s hour of weakness. Without her, it looked a vulgar scene enough in that little sitting-room, from which the smoke of Fred’s pipe had never fairly disappeared, and where Fred himself had lain in dismal state. Dr. Rider said a hasty good night to Fred’s successor, and went off hurriedly into the changed world which surrounded that unconscious cottage. Though the frost had not relaxed, and the air breathed no balm, no sudden leap from December to June could have changed the atmosphere so entirely to the excited wayfarer who traced back the joyful path towards the lights of Carlingford twinkling brilliant through the Christmas frost. As he paused to look back upon that house which now contained all his hopes, a sudden shadow appeared at a lighted window, looking out. Nettie could not see the owner of the footsteps which moved her to that sudden involuntary expression of what was in her thoughts, but he could see her standing full in the light, and the sight went to the doctor’s heart. He took off his hat insanely in the darkness and waved his hand to her, though she could not see him; and, after the shadow had disappeared, continued to stand watching with tender folly if perhaps some indication of Nettie’s presence might again reveal itself. He walked upon air as he went back, at last, cold but joyful, through the blank solitude of Grange Lane. Nothing could have come amiss to the doctor in that dawn of happiness. He could have found it in his heart to mount his drag again and drive ten miles in celestial patience at the call of any capricious invalid. He was half-disappointed to find no summons awaiting him when he went home﻿—no outlet for the universal charity and loving-kindness that possessed him. Instead, he set his easy-chair tenderly by the side of the blazing fire, and, drawing another chair opposite, gazed with secret smiles at the visionary Nettie, who once had taken up her position there. Was it by prophetic instinct that the little colonial girl, whose first appearance so discomposed the doctor, had assumed that place? Dr. Rider contemplated the empty chair with smiles that would have compromised his character for sanity with any uninstructed observer. When the mournful Mary disturbed his reverie by her noiseless and penitent entrance with the little supper which she meant at once for a peace-offering and compensation for the dinner lost, she carried downstairs with her a vivid impression that somebody had left her master a fortune. Under such beatific circumstances closed the evening that had opened amid such clouds. Henceforth, so far as the doctor could read the future, no difficulties but those common to all wooers beset the course of his true love.

===Chinese===

“可是明天，”医生说，把那只发颤的手紧紧握住。妮蒂绷得太紧、太激动，再说不出话来。她把手从他手里抽出去的时候，突然迸出一声孤零零的呜咽，随即像一个飞去的精灵似的消失了。医生看见了她的胸脯的起伏，看见了那已达极点的、再不能更进一层的自制。他像个真正的恋人似的回到客厅里去，不再窥探妮蒂软弱的一刻。少了她，那间小小的起坐室里的光景，看上去就够庸俗的了——弗雷德的烟斗冒的烟，从来不曾在那里真正散尽过，弗雷德本人也曾在那里凄凄惨惨地停过灵。莱德医生向弗雷德的后任匆匆道了晚安，便急急忙忙走进那个已经变样的世界——那世界围绕着这懵然无知的小屋。寒霜并未松劲，空气里也吸不到一点芳醇，可是对那位心潮激荡的旅人来说，纵然从十二月一跃而入六月，也不能把周围的气氛改变得这样彻底；他正循着那条欢欣的道路往回走，朝着卡林福德（Carlingford）穿过圣诞寒霜闪烁辉映的灯火。他停下来，回头望那座如今装着他全部希望的房子，一扇亮着灯的窗前蓦地出现一个影子，朝外张望。妮蒂看不见那阵脚步声的主人——正是那阵脚步声，逗得她不由自主地蓦然流露出她心里所想的东西；可他却看得见她整个儿立在灯光里，这一眼，直落进医生的心窝。他在黑暗里疯疯癫癫地摘下帽子，朝她挥动手臂，虽然她看不见他；等那影子消失了，他仍旧站着凝望，痴得那样温柔，巴望也许妮蒂在场的什么迹象会再一次显露出来。他终于往回走的时候，仿佛踏着云端，穿过格兰奇巷（Grange Lane）空荡荡的寂静——身上冷，心里却是欢喜的。在这幸福的黎明里，没有什么事能叫医生觉得不称心。倘若有哪个任性的病人来召唤，他满可以再套上他的轻便双轮马车，怀着天国似的耐心，赶十英里的路。回到家，没有出诊的召唤在等他——那洋溢在他胸中的、普施的仁爱与慈悲竟没有个出口——他倒有几分失望了。于是他把安乐椅温柔地安顿在熊熊的炉火旁边，又在对面拉过一把椅子，含着暗自的微笑，凝望那幻影中的妮蒂——她曾在那里站定过她的位置。那个初次露面便把医生搅得心慌意乱的小小的殖民地姑娘，莫非凭着先知的本能，占下了那个位置？莱德医生凝望着那把空椅子，满脸的笑，若是叫一个不明内情的旁观者看见，准会折损他神志清明的名声。那位愁容满面的玛丽（Mary）悄没声儿、满怀悔罪地端着小小的夜宵走了进来——那夜宵，她既当作求和的礼物，又当作失掉的晚饭的赔补——搅扰了他的出神；她走下楼去的时候，带着一个鲜明的印象：有人给她主人留下了一笔财产。那个在重重阴云之中开场的夜晚，就在这样的至福光景里闭幕了。从今以后，就医生所能望见的将来而言，横在他真情路上的，再没有别的困难，只有一切求爱的人共有的那些困难了。

### XVII / 十七

===Original===

When the red gleams of the early sunshine shone into that window from which Nettie had looked out last night, the wintry light came in with agitating revelations not simply upon another morning, but upon a new world. As usual, Nettie’s thoughts were expressed in things tangible. She had risen from her sleepless bed while it was still almost dark, and to look at her now, a stranger might have supposed her to be proceeding with her last night’s work with the constancy of a monomaniac. Little Freddy sat up in his crib rubbing his eyes and marvelling what Nettie could be about, as indeed anybody might have marvelled. With all those boxes and drawers about, and heaps of personal belongings, what was she going to do? She could not have answered the question without pain; but had you waited long enough, Nettie’s object would have been apparent. Not entirely free of that air of agitated haste﻿—not recovered from the excitement of this discovery, she was relieving her restless activity by a significant rearrangement of all the possessions of the family. She was separating with rapid fingers those stores which had hitherto lain lovingly together common property. For the first time for years Nettie had set herself to discriminate what belonged to herself from the general store; and, perhaps by way of softening that disjunction, was separating into harmonious order the little wardrobes which were no longer to be under her charge. Freddy opened his eyes to see all his own special belongings, articles which he recognised with all the tenacious proprietorship of childhood, going into one little box by themselves in dreadful isolation. The child did not know what horrible sentence might have been passed upon him while he slept. He gazed at those swift inexorable fingers with the gradual sob rising in his poor little breast. That silent tempest heaved and rose as he saw all the well-known items following each other; and when his last new acquisition, the latest addition to his wardrobe, lay solemnly smoothed down upon the top, Freddy’s patience could bear no more. Bursting into a long howl of affliction, he called aloud upon Nettie to explain that mystery. Was he going to be sent away? Was some mysterious executioner, black man, or other horrid vision of fate, coming for the victim? Freddy’s appeal roused from her work the abdicated family sovereign. “If I’m to be sent away, I shan’t go!” cried Freddy. “I’ll run off and come back again. I shan’t go anywhere unless you go, Nettie. I’ll hold on so fast, you can’t put me away; and, oh, I’ll be good!﻿—I’ll be so good!” Nettie, who was not much given to caresses, came up and put sudden arms round her special nursling. She laid her cheek to his, with a little outbreak of natural emotion. “It is I who am to be sent away!” cried Nettie, yielding for a moment to the natural bitterness.
